#5162. Binance CFO steps down nine days after record hack; co-CEO says transition was planned six months ago

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

After over five years at Binance, Kaiser Ng is leaving to "pursue a new venture." The firm's co-CEO said a transition plan had been in place for six months, which is long before the recent hack.

Binance Chief Financial Officer Kaiser Ng is stepping down and will be replaced by Justin Yek, a former finance executive at payments firm Airwallex, Binance co-CEO Yi He said in a statement on Saturday, Bloomberg reported.

Yi He said Ng "has decided to pursue a new venture" after more than five years at the company, and that a transition plan had been implemented over the past half year, according to Reuters. The statement did not say when Ng's departure takes effect or what his next venture is.

Ng has served as Binance's CFO since January 2022 and previously worked at rival exchange Kraken, according to his LinkedIn profile. Yek was most recently vice president of finance at payments firm Airwallex.

The announcement comes nine days after the Oct. 29 hack, in which attackers drained more than $6 billion from Binance across multiple blockchains. The FBI has attributed the attack to TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking group.

Binance had replenished about 95% of the affected assets with its own funds as of Nov. 2, when it completed a proof of reserves snapshot and began restoring withdrawals, The Block reported. The hackers have since swapped stolen tokens for $124 million in bitcoin, according to blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

#5161. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

A former FBI agent has pleaded guilty to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency using wallet credentials the bureau had collected during its own investigations, the Justice Department said on Thursday.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on March 4, 2027.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, pled guilty today to wire fraud and money laundering related to using his access to steal cryptocurrency.@FBIWFO https://t.co/jDVtSiAM3H

— U.S. Attorney EDVA (@EDVAnews) November 5, 2026

Yaroch worked at the FBI's Boston Division from 2017 to early 2025 on a squad investigating an unnamed "adversarial nation state," before moving to the bureau's Counterintelligence and Espionage Division, where he had access to FBI systems holding crypto credentials gathered in investigations, according to prosecutors.

Beginning no later than December 2024, he used that access to steal crypto from wallets controlled by subjects of other investigations, by nation-state actors, and from at least one wallet the FBI had created for an operation. He made about 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials from 35 separate FBI investigations, then swapped the stolen funds into other cryptocurrencies and moved them through further transactions to hide their origin. He used some of the money to pay off credit cards, car payments and other loans.

Yaroch also planned a new life abroad. He asked ChatGPT how to maximize profits from investments and where to live in the European Union, and after the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to obtain a tax identification number for him, prosecutors said. He arranged to travel to Portugal in September.

His actions compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, as well as sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection, according to the plea agreement's statement of facts.

Yaroch turned himself in. On July 29, he broke down while telling a former colleague at the Justice Department's National Security Division that he had made "very poor decisions" involving crypto wallets, according to an FBI affidavit filed in the case. He then self-reported to FBI headquarters, saying he had become frustrated that the bureau "could not or would not act" against an adversary's crypto accounts and had decided to "take matters into his own hands."

At the time, Yaroch said he had made about 10 to 12 transfers worth under $1 million, far less than the figures in his plea. Agents searching his home two days later seized a Trezor hardware wallet, three passports and a slip of paper bearing seed phrases, according to the affidavit. His Kraken account held about $188,600, mostly in U.S. dollars and the USDC stablecoin, with smaller amounts in Squid (QUID) and Bitcoin. A further $933,800 sat in Suilend, a lending protocol on the Sui blockchain, which he had accessed through the Slush wallet app. Yaroch told agents he chose Suilend "simply because he liked that the logo was a water droplet."

Agents transferred about $925,000 from those accounts to government-controlled wallets, the affidavit said. The roughly $165,600 held as U.S. dollars on Kraken could not be moved the same way.

Decrypt reported on the charges against Yaroch in August.

#5158. Thailand SEC Files Criminal Complaint Against Bitget Ahead of Dec. 5 Access Block

Thailand's SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget operator BG Limited for unlicensed Thai trading from 2022 to 2025.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission filed a criminal complaint Thursday against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, alleging it ran an unlicensed digital-asset exchange for Thai users from 2022 to 2025, and notified the government to block the platform from Dec. 5.

The complaint, lodged with the Economic Crime Suppression Division, also names Mr. Kritsana Kritsananuwat as an alleged supporter who promoted Bitget through Facebook and YouTube channels, the SEC said in statement No. 241/2026.

From 2022 through 2025, the regulator found, Bitget offered a trading system in the ordinary course of business, charged fees, ran a Thai-language website that could show values in baht, and solicited Thai users on social channels including “Bitget Thailand” on X, “BGB Thai Holder” on Telegram and “bitgetthailand” on TikTok.

That conduct amounts to operating a digital-asset exchange under Section 3 of the Emergency Decree on Digital Asset Businesses B.E. 2561 (2018) without a license, a violation of Section 26 punishable under Section 66, the SEC said. The case now moves to an inquiry official, then prosecutors and the courts.

Separately, the SEC submitted Bitget’s website, other domains and its app to the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society under a 2025 royal decree that authorizes access blocks on unlicensed trading platforms. From Dec. 5, the public “will be unable to access the platform,” the statement said, and investors still holding assets there were told to consider acting before the cutoff.

The enforcement push lands weeks after Bitget announced a planned acquisition of GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics (Thailand), a Bangkok digital-asset exchange and brokerage that already holds Thai licenses, through its interest in BTGT Management (Thailand) Ltd.

Under that deal, GMO Internet and its Thai holding company would transfer their entire stake in GMO-Z — about 49% and 50.94%, respectively — to BTGT or a designee at completion, a Sept. 24 GMO Internet filing showed. Bitget said completion remains subject to regulatory approval and, after a change-of-control nod, it intends to introduce its trading platform to Thai users under local rules.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said in the Oct. 20 release.

The timetable has already slipped. GMO Internet Group pushed the effective date of the share transfer from Oct. 31 to Nov. 30, citing careful work on Thai regulatory applications and closing procedures. That revised date sits five days before the planned Bitget access block.

CoinDesk reported that Bitget’s own terms of use, updated in October, already list Thailand among jurisdictions where its services are prohibited, and that the SEC has not said whether the criminal complaint will weigh on the GMO-Z change of control.

Thailand has used the same access-blocking tool before. In mid-2025 the SEC referred Bybit, OKX and three other unlicensed platforms for a June 28 cutoff, telling users to move assets first. The Bitget action follows that template — and adds a criminal complaint against the exchange operator and a named promoter.

Licensed venues still sit at the center of Bangkok’s policy mix. Thailand has approved a multi-year capital-gains tax break only for trades on SEC-regulated platforms, and has been expanding regulated crypto products in derivatives and capital markets. Unlicensed platforms get the opposite treatment: investor alerts, access blocks and, now for Bitget, a police referral.

What is not yet clear is whether a criminal case against BG Limited for the 2022–2025 period bars, slows or leaves untouched BTGT’s bid to take over a licensed local operator. The SEC statement does not mention GMO-Z. Bitget has not publicly answered the complaint in a statement posted with the acquisition release.

For Thai users still on Bitget, the practical clock is Dec. 5. For Bitget, the test is whether Nov. 30 closes a regulated door into Thailand — or whether the complaint filed Thursday makes that licensed path the next question the ECD and the SEC have to answer.

#5157. Vitalik Buterin says ETH should be valued as a store of value, not a fee-burning token

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The Ethereum co-founder said the case for valuing ETH as a fee-burning token "never was as strong as it seemed" and that maximizing fees would force Ethereum to operate like a major corporation.

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin said he believes ether Ethereum (ETH) should be treated primarily as a store of value, arguing that the case for valuing it as a fee-burning token was weaker than it appeared during the last market cycle.

In an X thread on Sunday morning, Buterin said "ETH as store of value" makes "much more sense to focus on" than "ETH as fee-burning token." He said EIP-1559, the 2021 upgrade that burns part of every transaction fee, "is fine and good," and the network should keep the mechanism and its burn, even if the burn only partially offsets the issuance of new ETH. "But we should not feel sad if fee burn is < 10% of breakeven indefinitely; the value of ETH comes from elsewhere," he wrote.

The fee burn was the basis of the "ultrasound money" thesis that gained traction among ETH holders during the 2021 bull market. Buterin, in his thread, laid out the valuation model behind fee-burning tokens, in which a token's market cap would equal the annual burn divided by a discount rate. Under that model, a token burning $1 billion a year at a 4% discount rate would have a natural valuation floor of $25 billion, he wrote.

"But here's the problem: this was never a true floor," Buterin wrote. He said most of the ETH burned in 2021 through 2023 came from "crypto-internal use cases" whose revenue would have fallen alongside ETH's price, citing the network "randomly [going] crazy" for a "digital monkey sale" that burned $180 million in fees in a single day. "The floor argument only works if the backing of the revenue stream is external," he wrote.

(Actually, the sale Buterin referenced was for digital land in the Otherside metaverse from Yuga Labs, the creators of the Bored Ape Yacht Club NFT collection. The sale saw users paying up to $10,000 in transaction fees for Otherdeed NFTs that currently sell for around $150.)

Buterin said Ethereum now has a "better claim to this external backing" because of the growing market for tokenized real-world assets. But as a "normal stable mature business," that activity is unlikely to produce the same fee spikes, he added.

The amount of ETH burned each day has fallen sharply since then, according to The Block's data. Ethereum burned about 1.48 million ETH in 2022, worth roughly $3.6 billion, compared with about 19,200 ETH, or $43 million, so far this year.

Buterin said that justifying ETH's market cap, which he put at about $270 billion, through fee burn alone would make Ethereum comparable to a company like L'Oreal. Ether's market cap stood at $273.3 billion on Sunday morning, according to The Block's ETH price page.

Companies of that size spend heavily to keep their positions, Buterin wrote. Citing an AI estimate that L’Oreal spends about 0.58% of its market cap on research and development each year, he said matching that rate would “bankrupt the [Ethereum Foundation] almost immediately,” so it would instead require in-protocol funding of about 700,000 ETH a year, cutting validator revenue by two-thirds.

What Buterin left unstated: the level of funding received by the Ethereum Foundation (EF) is already somewhat controversial in the crypto community.

The EF's ETH sales have regularly drawn criticism from holders, including after it sold 20,000 ETH worth about $47 million to Bitmine over two weeks in April and May. Buterin said in May that he regularly hears from community members who feel the EF's actions don't match its public commitments.

The EF began staking part of its treasury earlier this year to generate yield, and in March it published a mandate describing itself as "one of many stewards" of the network. Amid a wave of senior staff departures in May, Buterin said the foundation would become "a smaller ship" that sells less ETH and focuses on work that "would not happen otherwise."

Buterin said growth in assets issued natively on Ethereum's base layer, including real-world assets, is "highly synergistic" with the store-of-value case because it expands what holders can do with ETH, such as using it as collateral.

Buterin views Ethereum as "really not just a blockchain anymore," but rather "a hybrid architecture that combines together blockchains and modern cryptography." In September, he mapped out Ethereum's roadmap through 2030 and said the upcoming Hegota upgrade is likely to be the network's last "normal" fork.

Buterin's post garnered support from other influential figures in crypto. Solana co-founder Anatoly Yakovenko said Buterin "is absolutely right that you can't focus on fees as the product." Attorney Gabriel Shapiro of MetaLeX called the thread "a 'nature is healing' moment," while Moonrock Capital founder Simon Dedic argued that ETH as a store of value would be "the biggest bull case for Ethereum. And at the same time the easiest story to sell to institutional capital."

The thread comes about five weeks after the co-authors of controversial proposal EIP-8363 withdrew it from consideration for the network's forthcoming Hegota upgrade, which will follow the behind-schedule Glamsterdam hard fork. The proposal, which would have burned a rising share of validator rewards as more ETH is staked, cited the risk a high staking ratio poses to "ETH's role as money" and drew opposition from SharpLink CEO Joseph Chalom and Aave founder Stani Kulechov. Its authors plan to hold a separate issuance process that runs through EthCC in April.

ETH traded at $2,236 as of 9:23 a.m. ET, down 0.3% on the day, according to The Block's price page.

#5155. Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

📉 Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

On Wednesday, the market decided a Democratic Congress was good news. Stocks had their best day in three months, Bitcoin climbed back over $80,800, and Trump posted on Truth Social that the market “just hit another Record High” with 401(k)s “through the roof.”

On Thursday, the market changed its mind.

The S&P 500 fell 4.36%, its worst session since the Liberation Day tariffs in April 2025. The Nasdaq dropped 5.52% and closed in correction, more than 10% below its record from last month. The Dow shed 1,833 points.

The damage concentrated in one place. The iShares Semiconductor ETF fell more than 12%, its worst day since March 2020. Micron lost 17.5%, AMD 17.3%, Marvell 16.4%, Nvidia 9.3%. The Magnificent Seven shed roughly $1 trillion in market value. The VIX touched 28.09 intraday, its highest since March. By the close, the U.S. stock market had lost $3T in value - more than the entire market cap of crypto.

Notably, the thing that spooked investors was the same election result that lifted everything a day earlier. Several states where Democrats won have active local opposition to data center construction, and the market spent Thursday pricing in slower permitting and tighter oversight of the AI buildout. Ipek Ozkardeskaya at Swissquote put it simply: “We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout.” Rate expectations moved too, with December hike odds falling to 70.1% from 82.7%.

Crypto did not sit this one out. Bitcoin fell 5.4% to $75,871, below where it traded before the election. Ethereum dropped 7.6% to $2,239, a steeper fall than the Nasdaq. XRP lost 9.8%, and Hyperliquid fell 4.9%. The CMC20 index fell 6.29%. Almost $1.1B in crypto longs were liquidated on the day, before a slight relief rally overnight.

Friday’s October jobs report is next. After a week where the market read the same political outcome two opposite ways inside two sessions, it is fair to wonder how much anyone actually knows right now. We will find out soon…

#5153. Bitcoin Magazine Books Opens 333-Copy Genesis Book Collector Edition for 3rd Anniversary

Bitcoin Magazine Books opened a third-anniversary campaign Friday for Aaron van Wirdum’s The Genesis Book, offering a redesigned Collectors Edition limited to 333 hand-signed, individually numbered hardcovers in an exclusive Genesis Block slipcase.

Each Collectors Edition hardcover is hand-signed and hand-numbered by van Wirdum, the former Bitcoin Magazine editor-in-chief whose book traces the people, projects and ideas that preceded Satoshi Nakamoto’s peer-to-peer cash design. The standard package is priced at $221 and pairs the book with a limited trading card carrying a Certificate of Authenticity, a collector pin, a sticker sheet, the slipcase and a digital package.

Two one-of-one packages sit outside the ordinary number queue. The #1 package is listed at $5,000 and the #21 package at $2,500; each includes the matching numbered copy, a matching one-of-one collector card and the same accessories as the standard collector package.

Standard collector numbers are assigned in purchase order starting at #2 and skipping #21, the campaign FAQ says. Once preorders close, the signed Genesis print run will not be reproduced.

The Genesis Book Collector's Edition is live!333 copies.Individually numbered.Exclusive Genesis Block slipcase.Hand-signed by Aaron van Wirdum.Once they're gone, they're gone. 🚀 pic.twitter.com/BgiJoCVt8m

Beside the signed run, Bitcoin Magazine Books is selling a Premium Edition hardcover for $160 with foil stamping, a ribbon marker, stained page edges and custom endsheets. That edition is not limited, signed, numbered or slipcased, and it does not include the collector accessories or digital files.

A standalone Digital Package at $9.99 delivers the ebook in EPUB and the audiobook in MP4 immediately after purchase, independent of whether the physical campaign clears its funding bar.

Physical collector and premium orders are scheduled to ship in calendar year 2027.

Store images for the slipcase and accessories are artistic renderings; the campaign page notes that color, materials and finishes may vary in production. The campaign is operated by BTC Inc., which publishes Bitcoin Magazine.

#5151. Web3Auth says it frontran hackers by moving $5.6 million out of exposed Keplr wallets

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Consensys' Web3Auth team said it used a flaw that exposed some Keplr Wallet users' private keys to move their funds to safe addresses. Its 12 recovery wallets currently hold about $5.6 million.

Web3Auth, the Consensys-owned service that runs Google and Apple sign-in for crypto wallet Keplr, said it moved funds out of vulnerable Keplr wallets to keep them away from an attacker. The 12 addresses it says hold the recovered funds had about $5.6 million in crypto as of publicatino time on Sunday, according to onchain data reviewed by The Block.

A flaw in Web3Auth's systems exposed the private keys of some Keplr Wallet users who signed up with a Google or Apple account, the firms said. Though it was initially designed for the Cosmos ecosystem, Keplr now supports now supports many non-Cosmos chains, including EVM networks, and said in 2025 that it had more than 2 million users.

"This was not a hack of Keplr's code. The incident originated at Web3Auth, a provider Keplr uses," Keplr co-founder Josh Lee wrote on X, estimating that about 20% of Keplr's Google and Apple login accounts were affected. Users who set up Keplr with a recovery phrase or a hardware wallet are not affected, Keplr said.

Web3Auth said on Saturday it had started emptying vulnerable wallets the attacker hadn't reached yet, using "the same path of the original attacker." "Funds recovered through this process are safe, and we are working with Keplr on a path to recovery for users," Web3Auth said.

Web3Auth's reference to an "original attacker" suggests some wallets were drained before the recovery began. Web3Auth has not said how many wallets the attacker hit or how much was taken, and did not immediately respond to a request for further information from The Block.

"We are aware that some of these transactions have been mistaken for attacker activity," Web3Auth said early Sunday, when it published its recovery addresses. Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery had reported on Saturday that a thief collected about $6 million from more than 44,000 Keplr wallets. All eight addresses Bitquery tied to the thief are on Web3Auth's recovery list.

Most of the recovered funds are on Cosmos Hub, where Web3Auth's recovery address holds about 2.49 million ATOM Cosmos Hub (ATOM), worth roughly $3.3 million. Much of that ATOM had been staked, and the sweeps converted it into tokens that could be moved right away instead of waiting out the network's 21-day withdrawal period.

Lee said on Saturday that many vulnerable accounts may have dodged the attack. "It appears only a fraction of that 20% has actually been exploited, and the remaining accounts may be recoverable," he wrote. Keplr warned that some accounts "may already show a reduced or empty balance as a result of these mitigation efforts."

Keplr told Google and Apple login users not to send any funds to their accounts, advising them to move any remaining funds to a wallet secured by a recovery phrase or a hardware device. Keplr said it is working with Web3Auth on a way for users to check whether their accounts were affected.

Web3Auth first disclosed the issue on Friday afternoon and paused the service that lets apps create wallets through social logins. It said it had found no evidence that users of MetaMask, Consensys' flagship wallet, were affected. Consensys acquired Web3Auth in June 2025 and now markets the service as MetaMask Embedded Wallets, according to its developer documentation.

The Block has reached out to Web3Auth, Consensys and Keplr for comment.

#5150. Nigeria sets new crypto licensing rules, bans algorithmic stablecoins

Nigeria has banned algorithmic stablecoins and introduced reserve requirements for issuers as it expands central bank oversight of crypto payment services.

Nigeria’s central bank has introduced new licensing and reserve requirements for cryptocurrency companies, tightening oversight of stablecoins and digital asset payment services in one of Africa’s largest crypto markets.

The Central Bank of Nigeria (CBN) issued the Regulations for the Operations of Non-Security Virtual Assets on Thursday, establishing rules for stablecoin issuers, crypto payment providers, exchanges, custodians and other covered service providers.

The regulations took effect immediately, though existing operators have 180 days to apply for licenses or authorizations. The CBN said the framework aims to support responsible innovation while protecting consumers and financial stability.

The move follows President Bola Tinubu’s July executive order, which sought to coordinate crypto oversight among Nigerian authorities without changing their existing powers.

Under the new regulations, stablecoin issuers must fully back their tokens with reserve assets and maintain an additional buffer equal to at least 2% of their value. At least 40% of reserves must consist of cash or qualifying highly liquid assets.

The rules also set minimum paid-up capital of 10 billion naira ($7.4 million) for naira-backed issuers and 15 billion naira ($11.1 million) for foreign currency-backed stablecoin issuers.

Additionally, companies providing payment-related crypto services, including exchange and custody services, must maintain minimum paid-up capital of 1 billion naira ($742,000).

The regulations also prohibit stablecoins that rely primarily on algorithms rather than eligible reserves to maintain their value, along with tokens that lack enforceable redemption rights.

Nigeria has taken a restrictive approach to crypto trading, previously limiting banking access for exchanges and seeking to restrict peer-to-peer trading using local currency.

The country drew international attention in the crypto industry over its dispute with Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange by trading volume.

Binance discontinued its Nigerian naira services in March 2024 amid government scrutiny of crypto trading and allegations of currency manipulation. Nigerian authorities also detained Binance executive Tigran Gambaryan for nearly eight months before dropping money laundering charges against him in October 2024.

More recently, Nigeria’s revenue agency introduced crypto tax collection rules for digital asset platforms and peer-to-peer marketplaces in August 2026.

#5149. Tesla to Wind Down Cybertruck Production as Elon Musk Pivots to Robotaxis and AI

Tesla to Wind Down Cybertruck Production as Elon Musk Pivots to Robotaxis and AI

Tesla will stop making the Cybertruck at its Texas Gigafactory around the middle of next year, CEO Elon Musk said on Friday, as the company shifts its focus to autonomous vehicles.

"Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production," Musk tweeted.

Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production

Responding to a user who called the Cybertruck the best vehicle Tesla has ever made, Musk replied: "True, but the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time." "Cybertruck was an incredible technology development platform and lives on through Cybercab," Wes Morrill, the truck's lead engineer, wrote in response to Musk's post, thanking the truck's owners and "everyone who contributed to making the future looking like the future."

The stainless-steel pickup, first delivered to customers in late 2023, has struggled to win buyers. Tesla delivered 8,295 vehicles in its "other models" category, which includes the Cybertruck, in the third quarter, down 48% from a year earlier, Electrek reported. That puts the truck's sales at roughly a tenth of the 250,000 units a year that Tesla built its production line to make, according to the outlet.

Musk has made the Cybercab, Tesla's robotaxi, central to his push to turn the carmaker into an AI and robotics company. In April 2025, he said Tesla would launch its first robotaxi service in Austin using Model Y vehicles running its Full Self-Driving software, and begin building Cybercabs in Texas the following year.

Tesla began offering rides in the Cybercab, a two-seater with no steering wheel or pedals that was first unveiled in October 2024, in limited areas of Austin on September 3, Reuters reported. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration said at the time that it was "evaluating the situation," as federal rules typically require cars to have human controls. Tesla began producing the Cybercab in April, and had 45 of them registered in Texas as of the launch, according to the report.

At the 2024 unveiling, Musk said he expects the Cybercab to cost around $30,000, Auto Express reported.

The shift comes as Musk weighs combining Tesla with SpaceX, which absorbed his AI company xAI earlier this year. Last month, Musk said both companies' boards had formed special committees of independent directors to evaluate a merger, which would reshape his AI empire by bringing rockets, satellites, AI models, robots and cars under one roof. A SpaceX filing has since confirmed that any combination would need sign-off from its "controlling shareholder," Musk.

A merger would also create a Bitcoin treasury behemoth, combining SpaceX's 18,712 BTC and Tesla's 11,509 BTC for a total of 30,221 BTC, worth roughly $2.3 billion at current prices.

Both companies have also blocked their shares from being offered on the tokenized stock venue being built by crypto exchange OKX and NYSE owner ICE.

#5147. Onchain data casts doubt on insider-trading theory in NYT’s ‘suspicious’ election betting story

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

A New York Times story raised questions about a Polymarket trader's winning Senate bets, but The Block's analysis reveals a broader trading history that suggests other explanations for the wagers.

The New York Times wrote Friday that Vanderbilt University researchers had flagged large, profitable bets on Polymarket and Kalshi that were placed before certain polls on the Nebraska and Michigan Senate races were released. The polls favored the candidates the trader had backed, raising questions about whether the trader had advance access to the results.

However, The Block's own analysis of onchain records and trading data found that the trader has a long history of both large, risky trades and complex DeFi yield-farming that was unreported by the Times. Matching onchain deposits to Kalshi trades also suggests that the trader may have placed two large bets 10 minutes after one poll in question was published.

The trader held on to the bets all the way to Election Day rather than taking profits from short-term moves in the markets in the wake of the polls, and placed a bet on the race in Michigan while the Times/Siena polling was still underway, facts that the Times acknowledged in its report.

Those facts complicate the theory that the trader's profits resulted from advance access to unpublished Times/Siena polls. Two experts who reviewed The Block's findings said that while foul play can't be definitively ruled out, other explanations, like the trader being a crypto whale with a proclivity for large directional bets, fit the evidence better.

The Times story acknowledged it was unclear whether the trader had inside information. The researchers offered other possible explanations, and Polymarket told the paper it was conducting its own investigation.

Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College who reviewed The Block's findings, said the activity looked more like bets on the eventual election outcomes than an attempt to profit from the publication of the Times/Siena polls.

"I think these were just directional bets. Maybe they were based on non-public information such as privately commissioned surveys, but I don't think the Times/Siena polls were the information source," Sethi said.

The Vanderbilt researchers identified a Polymarket user who bet $191,277 that Sen. Pete Ricketts, a Nebraska Republican, would win re-election. Most of that money was wagered in the early hours of Oct. 31, roughly an hour before a Times/Siena poll published at 5:05 a.m. ET showed Ricketts ahead by six points. The user ultimately made more than $77,000 on the race, according to the Times.

The same user bet $622,608 on a Democratic victory in Michigan's Senate race. At around 7:53 p.m. ET on Nov. 1, the Times published polls of Michigan and six other states showing Michigan Democrat Abdul El-Sayed ahead by 11 points. The user made more than $180,000 on that race when El-Sayed won the seat.

The Block's analysis of Polymarket's public records matched those bets to an account named dubdubdub2. On Oct. 31, it bought $349,135 of "Yes" shares on a Democratic win in Michigan at 3:27 a.m. and $273,473 of "No" shares on a Republican win at 5:20 a.m., which are essentially bets in the same direction. Together, the two bets match the Michigan total reported by the Times.

The bets were unusually large for those markets. The account's Michigan purchases were the largest daily total by one account in any of the 16 Senate races The Block analyzed back to mid-September, and were nearly three times the next largest.

"There's a series of circumstances here that it's hard to write it off as a random occurrence," Brett Goldstein, who co-leads the prediction markets research team at Vanderbilt's Institute of National Security, told the Times. He said the activity "requires further investigation."

But while the Polymarket trades stood out on the platform, The Block's review of the trader's onchain history suggests the size of the bets was not unusual for the trader, and the specific details of some trades cut against the theory that they were exploiting advance knowledge of the polls.

Michigan also wasn't the race the Nov. 1 polls moved most. That release covered seven states, and the biggest market reaction came in Kansas, where Polymarket's odds of a Democratic win jumped from about 52.5% to 60.5% within half an hour. In Michigan, where Democrats were already the strongest favorites at about 74.5%, the odds rose to about 79.5% over two hours.

The trader did not bet on Kansas. Its market was much smaller, however, with about $1.9 million traded on a Democratic win through the election, compared with about $7.9 million in Michigan.

The researchers told the Times that the trader sent large sums to Kalshi and that large bets on similar markets followed, but the report didn't identify those bets.

The Block traced funds from wallets linked to the Polymarket account to a crypto payment provider used by Kalshi, then compared those deposits with Kalshi's public trade records. The records suggest the trader was betting on the same races on both platforms in the same directions, including possible bets placed after the Nebraska poll became public.

The account's Senate bets were funded by a separate wallet that also traded on Hyperliquid. The Block linked that wallet to the one controlling dubdubdub2 through a history of transfers between connected addresses. Its five transfers to dubdubdub2's Polymarket deposit address on Oct. 30 and 31, totaling $850,000, also match the account's deposit records to the dollar.

At 5:29 p.m. ET on Oct. 30, the betting wallet withdrew its remaining $2.2 million from Hyperliquid, where it had been trading over the previous day. About a minute later, it began sending funds to a deposit address that forwards money to a wallet labeled by blockchain analytics firm Arkham and Arbiscan as belonging to Zero Hash.

Kalshi lists Zero Hash among its crypto deposit providers. The betting wallet sent about $1.39 million to the deposit address through Oct. 31. The Block found no earlier transfers to Zero Hash from the trader's known wallets.

Kalshi's data does not link specific trades to specific accounts. However, since the transfers were consistent with Kalshi deposits and six-figure bets were rare in Kalshi's Nebraska and Michigan Senate markets, The Block was able to match certain deposits to trades by timing and size.

Several of the trader's Zero Hash deposits were followed within roughly a minute by large purchases in Kalshi's Nebraska Senate markets. On Oct. 30, a $175,000 deposit at 5:30:08 p.m. was followed 31 seconds later by about $159,000 in purchases backing Ricketts at 66 cents. A further $157,000 deposit at 5:31:58 p.m. was followed at 5:33:29 p.m. by about $169,000 in bets against Osborn at 67 cents. Both positions backed the same election outcome.

At about 5:15 a.m. on Oct. 31, 10 minutes after the Times/Siena poll was published, Kalshi recorded about $153,000 in purchases backing Ricketts to win and independent Dan Osborn to lose. The two groups of trades came eight seconds apart at prices around 75 cents, echoing the paired purchases seen earlier.

These trades are harder to attribute to the trader, however. They came about 50 minutes after a $250,000 deposit, while several earlier purchases followed deposits within roughly a minute. The deposit could also have funded bets in other markets.

If the trader did make those bets, continuing to buy in after the poll was published would suggest it was betting on the election outcome generally rather than trying to profit from the poll's short-term effect on the market, though late buys do not completely rule out the trader having advance knowledge of the poll.

Onchain transfers suggest the trader may have made more from its Kalshi activity than the roughly $258,000 its Polymarket Senate positions earned. On Nov. 4 and 5, its wallet received about $1.98 million from Zero Hash-labeled wallets, roughly $590,000 more than it had sent to Zero Hash in the first place, presumably as Kalshi deposits.

The transfers alone don't show exactly how much the trader made on Kalshi. The difference between the funds deposited and the funds received could reflect winning bets in other markets, trades that are missing from Kalshi's public records or money the trader originally deposited via a bank account.

The Times reported that the user had traded in just three Polymarket markets before October, but its story did not detail what those trades were. The Block's review found those positions were also substantial, and that holding through settlement was a recurring strategy for the trader.

The trader's dubdubdub2 account bet about $1.05 million on Zohran Mamdani winning the 2025 New York City mayoral election, about $102,000 against Andrew Cuomo in the same race and about $131,000 against the San Francisco 49ers winning the 2026 Super Bowl. It held all three positions until the markets resolved. The Block did not identify any bets that were placed shortly before mayoral polls were published.

Vincent Grégoire, a finance professor at HEC Montréal whose research includes insider trading, described the trader's history as "consistent with a wealthy individual (or small trading firm) who likes to take large bets on various platforms."

While the Times story notes that the trades were made early in the morning U.S. time, The Block's review of roughly two years of activity beginning in October 2024 found that 3 a.m. ET was the busiest hour for wallets linked to the trader. The analysis excluded Oct. 29 through Nov. 5, 2026, so the Senate bets themselves did not influence the result. It covered activity on Ethereum, Arbitrum, Base, Polygon, Hyperliquid and Polymarket, plus Solana bridge transfers.

The trader also has extensive experience within decentralized finance outside prediction markets, per The Block's analysis. The linked wallets have repeatedly farmed lending and yield protocols such as Aave and Pendle, adding up to tens of millions of dollars in total transfer volume. They have withdrawn more than $13 million in stablecoins from Kraken since October 2025. A linked Solana wallet also traded memecoins heavily in early 2025, including the official TRUMP token on its launch weekend.

The trader has also seen its share of trades gone wrong. Immediately before the election bets, the betting wallet deposited about $2.99 million in USDC on Hyperliquid and lost about $783,000 over 26 hours, including fees and funding costs, according to Hypurrscan's records for the account. The losses included about $217,000 on HYPE spot trading, $234,000 on a leveraged HYPE long and $311,000 on leveraged Intel perpetual futures positions opened after Intel reported third-quarter results.

The wallet behind dubdubdub2 also bought 60.43 million WLFI tokens for about $906,500 on the first day of the October 2024 public token sale by World Liberty Financial, the crypto venture owned by President Donald Trump's family, a purchase the Times also noted. The purchase made the wallet the largest buyer in the sale that month.

The Times said the purchase accounted for nearly one-third of all tokens sold that October. Onchain records reviewed by The Block instead put its share at about 6% of the roughly 970 million WLFI sold by the project that month, or about 9% of first-day sales. The Block has asked the Times about the discrepancy.

Grégoire said in an interview with The Block that large election bets can reflect a trader's forecasting models, private polls or other sources of information.

"To me, the simplest explanation is that there's no foul play," he said. "It's just someone that has other sources or other models."

Grégoire questioned why a polling employee would place a bet worth hundreds of thousands of dollars ahead of publication and then leave the position open rather than taking the immediate profit. He also noted that some of Polymarket's biggest historical winners made their money through a small number of large presidential election bets.

Sethi also said the use of Kalshi, which verifies traders' identities, suggests "they were not too concerned with concealing their identity."

Though The Block's analysis casts doubt on the idea that the trader had advance access to the Times/Siena polls, the reasons for the trader's bets cannot be established from the data alone.

"It's almost impossible to tell from the data; you have to know who was trading and on what information," Grégoire said, noting that it's reasonable for the institutions involved to investigate whether their processes had been compromised.

A Polymarket spokeswoman told the Times that the company spotted the trades immediately and is investigating. Kalshi declined to comment to the Times.

The Times also reported that spokespeople for the Times and the Siena Research Institute said they took the allegations seriously and planned to review the research. Times spokesperson Charlie Stadtlander said the newspaper prohibits employees from using unpublished coverage to make investments.

Lyle Durbin, CEO of ReconMR, the data collection firm that owns the Siena Research Institute, said employees are bound by confidentiality and nondisclosure agreements prohibiting the use of unpublished data for any purpose, according to the Times.

World Liberty Financial declined to comment through a representative. Kathleen Clark, a spokesperson for Vanderbilt's Institute of National Security, told The Block that Goldstein would not be able to comment. The Block also sought comment from Polymarket, Kalshi, the Times and Siena but has not heard back as of publication time.

The Times' scrutiny comes as prediction markets face broader questions about their level of oversight over their own platforms. Both Kalshi and Polymarket tightened their insider trading rules earlier this year amid scrutiny from the Senate.

In April, the Justice Department charged U.S. Army soldier Gannon Ken Van Dyke with using classified information about the operation to capture former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro to make about $409,881 on Polymarket. A former White House teleprompter operator agreed in August to pay $172,000 to settle CFTC charges that he used advance knowledge of Trump's speeches to bet on Kalshi's "mention markets."

An editor checks the facts, figures and quotes they produce against primary sources, and every piece is reviewed by an editor before it is published. Sponsored content and press releases are labeled as such and are not written by our editorial team.

#5145. U.S. Treasury Cuts Off MEXC From U.S. Financial System Over Alleged $16 Billion in Illicit Flows

The U.S. Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued a final order Friday that will cut offshore exchange MEXC Global off from the American banking system over alleged links to sanctions evasion by Iran, North Korea and Russia, the department said.

FinCEN’s order, published as a PDF pending Federal Register placement, finds MEXC a “primary money laundering concern in connection with Russian illicit finance” under section 9714(a) of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act. Thirty days after that Register notice, covered U.S. financial institutions will be barred from any funds transfer — including convertible virtual currency — to or from MEXC.

Reuters reported the same cut-off Friday afternoon. Treasury framed the move as part of Operation Economic Outcast. It is not an OFAC sanctions designation: the order says it does not rewrite Bank Secrecy Act duties or OFAC rules, and it is a FinCEN funds-transmittal ban rather than a Section 311 proposed rulemaking.

Treasury alleges MEXC facilitated at least $16 billion in illicit flows from May 2021 through May 2026, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion and about $22 million linked to Iran’s Central Bank and the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps’ Qods Force. The platform was still processing trades with sanctioned Garantex and successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after OFAC designated Garantex, the release says.

The order, signed by FinCEN Deputy Director Jimmy L. Kirby, covers MEXC Global and named affiliates operating outside the United States, including Seychelles-incorporated MEXC Global Ltd / MX Global Ltd, Estonia’s MEXC Estonia OÜ, Switzerland’s GIDA Technology AG and the U.K.’s MXC Global Ltd, plus successors. Covered institutions face civil or criminal penalties for willful violations.

Until the Federal Register publishes the notice, the 30-day clock has not started. As of Friday evening eastern time the Register’s public index still showed no MEXC document, matching the order’s own cover note that publication is pending.

Founded in 2018 and formerly branded MXC, MEXC ranks among the world’s 20 largest centralized crypto venues by volume. Treasury puts daily turnover above $1 billion and reserves near $5 billion as of mid-2026, with access claimed across more than 170 countries. The exchange already lists the United States as a prohibited jurisdiction in its user terms, so Friday’s action mainly hits any remaining dollar rails, banking partners and counterparties that still touch U.S. institutions.

Regulators elsewhere have been circling for years. The U.K. Financial Conduct Authority put MEXC Global Ltd on its warning list in March 2024 as unauthorized. Hong Kong’s Securities and Futures Commission warned the same month that the platform was marketing to local investors without a licence. Seychelles’ Financial Services Authority said in May 2026 it took action against MX Global Ltd for unlicensed virtual-asset operations — footnoted in FinCEN’s order.

Those compliance gaps matter because the illicit-flow thesis is specific, not guilt by association. Beyond Russia, FinCEN cites about $1 billion in direct traffic with more than 40 Iranian crypto exchangers, nearly $26 million with the OFAC-sanctioned A7 sanctions-evasion network, and almost $200 million tied to the A7A5 stablecoin. DPRK facilitators and cybercrime flows are also alleged. MEXC has not issued a public response to the Friday order; its main X account kept posting marketing content through the afternoon.

Practically, once the order bites, U.S. banks, money transmitters and other covered firms cannot move dollars or crypto to MEXC wallets or accounts. Offshore users may keep trading peer-to-peer, but dollar on-ramps and any bank that still clears for MEXC become legal risk. The template tracks earlier 9714 cut-offs of venues such as Bitzlato, and it lands beside a busy sanctions week that has already seen North Korea crypto designations, an Iranian exchange action, and Russia’s push to put bitcoin trading on the Moscow Exchange.

Whether MEXC contests the finding, seeks an exemption, or quietly reroutes liquidity is the open question. For now the paper trail is clear: a final FinCEN order under 9714(a), a 30-day fuse after Federal Register publication, and Treasury’s claim of at least $16 billion in illicit volume sitting behind the cut-off.

#5144. What Democrats’ US midterm sweep means for the CLARITY Act

The CLARITY Act faces a new test as Democrats control Congress, raising questions over consumer protections, ethics and what the crypto industry may have to concede.

Crypto companies contributed $233 million to political committees during the 2026 US midterm cycle, according to Public Citizen’s analysis of federal filings.

Democrats won both chambers of Congress and will take control in January, giving them a chance to reshape the stalled CLARITY Act, a bill to set federal rules for crypto markets.

In Ohio, Democrat and longtime crypto skeptic Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted despite crypto-backed political action committees spending $64 million to defeat him.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, one of crypto’s most vocal critics in Washington and the most senior Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, is expected to chair the panel in the next Congress.

Stronger consumer protections, tougher scrutiny of political conflicts and a shift in regulatory power are all likely to be on the docket in the next round of negotiations.

But a Democratic majority doesn’t have to kill the prospect of crypto market structure legislation completely.

Democrat Congressman Shri Thanedar remains hopeful that the CLARITY Act can still pass, but says any legislation must include stronger ethics requirements. He tells Magazine:

Can the industry’s political influence still be converted into legislation, and what will it have to concede to get there?

The Senate failed to advance the CLARITY Act in September, with disagreements over ethics provisions contributing to the impasse.

The crypto spent $233 million on the 2026 US midterm elections. Source: Public Citizen

Democrats are already reportedly discussing how to reshape the market structure debate, with House Democrats said to be considering expanding proposals from Representative Maxine Waters into a broader market structure bill.

Related: Trump’s memecoin ventures derailed CLARITY Act, Scaramucci says

That bill could include stronger consumer protections, a greater role for the Securities and Exchange Commission, and new ethics restrictions on public officials’ crypto activities.

That could finally mean separating the industry’s push for clearer rules from the controversies surrounding Trump’s personal crypto ventures.

Democrats have already signaled plans to investigate the Trump family’s business dealings and other corporate interests, with incoming House Oversight Committee Chair Robert Garcia planning to pursue inquiries into corporate corruption.

Hadley Stern, head of institutions at Silence Laboratories and founder of Fidelity Digital Assets, says Democrats are right to “go after” the second, and the industry should want them to; that’s “what’s poisoning the bipartisan deal,” he says.

“A sitting president’s family launching memecoins and a stablecoin is a real governance problem,” he tells Magazine. “If I’d done that at Fidelity, I’d have been walked out the same afternoon.”

He says 2026’s digital asset industry is very different from the last time Democrats “held the gavel,” pointing to its growth from “FTX and a bunch of tokens” in 2022 to a federal law for stablecoins, Treasuries and credit funds onchain and tokenized equities on the way:

The industry may also have to accept more demanding standards if it wants legislation that can survive changes in political control.

Paul Brody, founder and CEO of Nightfall Networks and former global blockchain lead at EY, expects banks “to come out winners,” as they will “push for crypto companies” to be held to the same exacting consumer protection and security standards.

Related: Crypto Biz: CLARITY Act setback puts Coinbase in the spotlight

He says the industry has opposed this, but tells Magazine that “a wise strategy” would be to “accept similar conditions,” given that blockchain-native ecosystems are still “vastly more flexible, programmable and efficient.”

Lindsay Fraser, chief policy officer at Blockchain Association, also argues consumer protection and innovation should not be treated as competing objectives.

“We can protect consumers while ensuring the law reflects how the technology actually works,” she says, adding the goal is “lasting policy that earns public trust, gives builders a future in this country, and strengthens American financial leadership.”

That doesn’t mean it’s all smooth sailing to a potential compromise.

Brody says there’s still the possibility of a presidential veto, and points out that crypto is likely to be a “low priority” compared to “investigations of corruption and battles over budgets and the war.”

The midterm results came as lawmakers were already grappling with the fallout from the Binance hack, the largest hack in crypto history, with over $6.3 billion stolen on Oct. 29, raising fresh concerns about exchange security and customer protections.

Thanedar says while the legislation “wouldn’t solve every potential problem,” it would provide “much-needed certainty” and “security guardrails,” and the hack provides a “clear example of the danger of not enacting appropriate legislation.”

Maylea Ma, deputy general counsel at 1inch, says rather than making a stronger case for passing the latest draft of CLARITY, the incident “highlights problems the legislation does not address.”

The hack involved a “centralized exchange’s hot wallet and signing failure,” Ma says, but the current draft “has no insurer, no loss payee, and no hot wallet cap that would have mitigated the loss for Binance or imposed security standards that would have prevented the hack.”

Amanda Fischer, policy director at Better Markets, said the episode highlighted how “unfit for purpose the crypto ecosystem is for mainstream finance,” and crypto exchanges should not act as custodians, since it creates a “honeypot risk.”

Binance hack shows how ‘unfit’ crypto is for mainstream finance. Source: Amanda Fischer

Ma says the incident is “likely to impact” the next vote on CLARITY and could make lawmakers less willing to pass legislation the industry wants, unless it comes with stronger consumer protections.

Crypto spent years convincing Washington it deserves a seat at the table; now it will need to agree on the obligations that come along with it.

#5143. $3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

$3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

Around half of the $6.43 billion stolen from Binance last month has yet to be laundered, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said in a report updated on Friday.

About $3.11 billion remains in addresses linked to the theft, mostly in ETH and Bitcoin, and those funds are "very likely" still held by North Korea, Elliptic said. A further $2.85 billion, or 44% of the haul, has been frozen or seized, mostly stablecoins that issuers such as Tether and Circle froze within an hour of the theft. That compares with around 3% frozen after the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, according to Elliptic.

Binance hack update: the XRP has started moving. About $50 million has been swapped, mostly through THORChain into BTC, with about $226 million still to move.

Bitcoin converted from other stolen assets is now about 1,650 BTC ($124 million), almost twice the figure a day earlier.…

The FBI has attributed the October 29 theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor. North Korea has denied involvement.

The laundering so far has been "highly complex and clearly automated," Elliptic said. It has used more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions, roughly one new wallet every 30 seconds.

The 18,270 BTC stolen from Binance, worth about $1.5 billion at the time of the theft, was split into 19 addresses on the day of the hack and has not moved since. The nine largest Ethereum addresses, holding 89% of the attacker's ETH, have not moved since October 30.

Most of the laundering so far, about $220 million in TRX, has run through Tron, where a single distribution wallet sent 684.6 million TRX to hundreds of new addresses over 51 hours, including through the night in Pyongyang. Almost all of it was bridged to Ethereum.

All of the stolen Solana, about $87 million at current prices, has been swapped, bridged or mixed, and the attacker has started laundering the stolen XRP, with about $50 million converted so far and $226 million remaining. In total, about $124 million has been converted into Bitcoin, much of it through cross-chain service THORChain.

The speed has fueled speculation that AI agents are moving the funds, but Elliptic said the evidence points to conventional automation. "The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," the firm said.

Elliptic expects the attacker to swap the ETH for Bitcoin and then cash out through OTC brokers and exchanges over a period of months. After the Bybit hack, North Korea laundered all 499,000 stolen ETH within 10 days but took months to cash out, the firm noted.

Binance has continued to restore services. On Friday, the exchange said users could now verify that their balances are backed at least 1:1 using the zero-knowledge proof for its latest proof-of-reserves snapshot, and that it aims to reopen withdrawals for all tokens on restored networks by the end of next week.

#5141. What is Zcash (ZEC)? How shielded transactions work

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Zcash, on the other hand, gives users the option to process their transactions privately. It offers transparent transactions, which function like a normal BTC transaction, as well as shielded transactions, which allow users to keep transaction details private through advanced cryptography.

The network launched in October 2016, and the nonprofit Zcash Foundation was established the following year to support the network and its development. Zcash has since evolved into a decentralized ecosystem spanning multiple independent organizations and contributors. The Zcash Foundation continues to maintain core infrastructure and support protocol development.

Zcash’s shielded transactions make use of cryptographic proofs to verify transactions are legitimate while keeping the underlying information concealed. This means that the transaction amount, sender’s address and recipient’s address are all kept private.

To understand how this works, it helps to look at how Zcash handles private funds. Transactions on the network are processed in two parallel but separate accounting environments: the transparent ledger and shielded pools. The latter is where private transactions take place; shielded pools are parts of the chain where funds can be held and transferred without their ownership, balance and history being publicly visible.

According to figures displayed on the official Zcash network dashboard, approximately 29% of the issued ZEC supply was held in shielded pools as of October 2026. It is also worth noting that transactions moving between transparent and shielded pools can reveal the amounts entering or leaving those pools, which in turn could be used to infer relationships between addresses.

While the regular blockchain uses conventional public addresses, shielded pools instead represent funds as cryptographic records called notes. When a user processes a shielded payment, their wallet uses existing notes to create new ones for the recipient. This transaction is recorded on the Zcash blockchain, but observers cannot see who received the notes, which notes were spent or how much ZEC changed hands.

A zero-knowledge proof (ZKP) is a cryptographic method that allows someone to prove a statement is true without revealing the underlying information used to establish it. Imagine if someone wanted to prove they were over 18 without revealing their exact date of birth — ZKPs are able to achieve a similar effect by using cryptographic mathematics.

Instead of broadcasting all of the information needed to verify a payment, the sender generates a cryptographic proof of its validity. Other users can view and verify that proof — the product of the cryptographic algorithm — while the information used to generate it is kept concealed.

Zcash uses a specific type of proof called a zero-knowledge succinct non-interactive argument of knowledge, or zk-SNARK. It also makes use of cryptographic identifiers called nullifiers attached to notes to prevent double-spending.

Viewing keys introduce another level of control for Zcash users. These allow them to selectively reveal transaction data with third parties while keeping it hidden from the general public. These viewing keys give limited read-only access to the wallet without giving the holder the capability to spend the balance inside.

The network allows for different levels of visibility. For example, an incoming viewing key reveals only received payments, while a full viewing key reveals broader details of an account’s shielded activity.

This is known as selective disclosure, allowing users to choose when and with whom they share financial information. Custom controls like this distinguish Zcash from networks like Monero (XMR), where all activity is anonymous by default.

Zcash supports transparent addresses that operate similarly to Bitcoin addresses and shielded addresses designed to protect transaction details. Unified addresses simplify the process by allowing users to manage both types of transaction from inside one wallet.

Sender, recipient and transaction amounts are publicly visible

zs1 for Sapling; newer shielded receivers are incorporated into unified addresses

Conceals transaction details when used in fully shielded transfers

This convoluted process was streamlined by successive network upgrades:

The upgrade also restricted activity in the original Orchard pool and introduced a mechanism known as a turnstile, allowing existing funds to migrate to Ironwood while enforcing supply-integrity checks. Compatible wallets can handle this migration without requiring users to create a new wallet.

New ZEC coins enter circulation through proof-of-work mining. This is the same system used by Bitcoin, wherein miners dedicate computing power to solving complex mathematical problems, with the successful miners gaining the right to produce new blocks that are in turn validated by other nodes. The specific algorithm used by Zcash is called Equihash.

ZEC also shares a similar emission schedule with BTC, with a fixed supply cap of 21 million coins and a halving in new emissions roughly every four years. The most recent Zcash halving occurred in November 2024, cutting the block subsidy (the network reward paid to miners) to 1.5625 ZEC.

Unlike Bitcoin, however, Zcash also directs a portion of newly minted coins toward network development.  A new funding model was introduced in November 2025 which now sees 80% of block subsidies paid to miners, 8% to community grants and 12% to a development fund controlled by ZEC holders.

Ring signatures, stealth addresses, RingCT

Not supported as a standard transaction mode

It’s important to note that neither ZEC nor XMR guarantee complete anonymity. Zcash balances can be tracked while moving in or out of private pools, while wallets on both networks can potentially be de-anonymized if they interact with a user’s centralized exchange accounts.

No. Shielded transactions can conceal the sender, recipient and amount, but Zcash does not guarantee complete anonymity. Transparent transactions remain publicly visible, while exchange records, network information and user behavior can reveal identifying details.

2. What is the difference between shielded and transparent Zcash transactions?

Transparent transactions publicly reveal wallet addresses and transaction amounts, much like Bitcoin. Shielded transactions use zero-knowledge proofs to conceal this information while allowing the network to verify the payment. Transfers between transparent and shielded pools can still reveal the amounts entering or leaving those pools.

Zcash ownership and use are generally permitted in many jurisdictions, although restrictions vary by country. Some jurisdictions, such as the EU, have introduced legislation restricting cryptocurrency platforms from maintaining anonymous accounts. Although not a blanket ban, this could limit the accessibility of ZEC when it takes effect in July 2027.

Zcash has a maximum supply of 21 million ZEC, matching BTC’s supply cap. New coins are issued through proof-of-work mining, with the block subsidy periodically reduced through halvings. The latest halving occurred in November 2024.

#5140. Strategy (MSTR) Adds 1,036 Bitcoin for $81.9 Million as Holdings Slip Below Cost

Strategy bought 1,036 bitcoin last week for $81.9 million at an average of $79,012, lifting its stack to 850,364 BTC.

Strategy bought another 1,036 bitcoin last week. The Saylor-led firm spent $81.9 million at an average of $79,012 per coin between November 2 and November 8, a Monday 8-K filing shows, lifting total holdings to 850,364 BTC bought for $64.16 billion at an average of $75,456.

The coins were funded with net proceeds from MSTR common stock sales. Strategy sold about 2.05 million shares for $283.8 million, put $81.9 million into bitcoin, spent $188.3 million buying back its STRC preferred and parked $13.6 million in USD cash. No preferred stock was sold under the ATM during the week.

That buyback retired about 1.89 million STRC shares. Strategy still has $560.3 million left under its preferred repurchase program and $1.0 billion under its MSTR buyback authorization, with about $18.3 billion of MSTR still available under the ATM.

Executive Chairman Michael Saylor posted the update on X: “Strategy has acquired 1,036 $BTC and repurchased $188M of $STRC. As of 11/8/26, we hold 850,364 BTC and $4.9B of USD Assets.”

On Sunday he shared his usual “dots” tracker chart with the line “Connecting the dots,” a tease that has often preceded Monday buy filings. The chart landed after a weekend slide that pushed bitcoin toward $74,000, its weakest level since August.

The filing lands as the spot bitcoin price sits below Strategy’s average cost. At the time of writing it trades near $74,800, about 0.9% under the $75,456 basis, leaving the stack roughly $550 million underwater on paper. The prior week’s average cost was $75,451, so the new purchase nudged the basis up by only $5 even though those 1,036 coins were bought at nearly $79,000.

Strategy’s ledger shows that was not the first time the firm has run a thin cushion this year. Between June 30 and August 10 it sold bitcoin in four consecutive filings — 1,363, then 2,225, then 1,638, then 1,690 coins — to fund preferred dividends and STRC buybacks before flipping back to accumulation.

Those sales cut the stack to 840,447 BTC at their low. Strategy has since rebuilt past that mark and now holds more than 4% of bitcoin’s 21 million supply cap. The pace of buying has slowed since the summer sales. Strategy added 811 bitcoin the week before this one and skipped buys entirely the week of October 19 through 25 while spending about $205 million on STRC.

STRC is designed to trade near its $100 par. Strategy has spent more than $2 billion retiring the preferred this year as the shares oscillated around that level, and shareholders recently approved daily dividends on STRC, STRF, STRK and STRD. Daily accrual for STRC began November 1.

This week’s mix put more ATM cash into preferred buybacks than into bitcoin. The $188.3 million STRC repurchase was more than twice the $81.9 million bitcoin spend, a pattern that has become familiar since Strategy built its USD Reserve and USD Cash buckets over the summer. Last week’s 811-coin purchase was also paired with a larger STRC buyback, then funded from USD cash rather than fresh stock sales.

As of November 8, the USD Reserve stood at $4.77 billion and USD Cash at $146.2 million. Strategy used $21.1 million of the reserve during the week to pay preferred dividends. The SEC 8-K is the primary disclosure; Strategy also posts the same figures on its public dashboard.

MSTR closed Friday at $128.93 after a rough stretch that included a 9% weekly drop into the October 30 close, and the stock was indicated lower in premarket trading Monday. At the time of writing, bitcoin is near $74,800, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows. The test now is whether Strategy keeps buying through an underwater stack or leans harder on preferred buybacks while the ATM stays open.

Anthropic IPO Valuation Could Be Slashed in Half to $1T After AI Stock Rout: Report

Major investors are increasingly doubtful that Anthropic's initial public offering can go ahead at its expected valuation of $1.5 trillion, after a sharp sell-off in AI stocks, The Wall Street Journal reported on Thursday.

Anthropic, which filed last month to list on Nasdaq under the ticker "ANT," has been aiming to go public the week before Thanksgiving and raise up to $100 billion, according to the Journal. But three major investors who plan to take part in the IPO told the paper the valuation will likely need to come down, with one suggesting it could be closer to $1 trillion.

The company could also raise less than planned, perhaps around $80 billion, one investor told the Journal. Another wondered whether Anthropic might delay the offering until market conditions improve. Anthropic executives and advisers are expected to meet in the coming days to discuss the plans, according to the report.

The doubts follow a rout in AI stocks on Thursday, as investors weighed whether Democratic gains in the midterms could bring stricter oversight of data center construction. The S&P 500 closed down 4.4% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq 100 fell 6.3%, both their worst days since April 2025, while the Philadelphia semiconductor index slid 12.3% and Nvidia dropped 9.2%, NBC News reported. Stocks partly recovered on Friday morning: as of 11:21 a.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite was up 1.4% and Nvidia had gained 3.4%, according to Yahoo Finance.

Democrats are planning bills to shield consumers from rising electricity costs tied to data centers, require independent testing of advanced AI systems and clarify when AI companies can be held liable for harm, Reuters reported, though any legislation would likely face steep odds while Trump remains in the White House. The party also plans closer scrutiny of leading AI companies, including Anthropic, according to the report.

Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-VT) has called for a "national data center moratorium," while Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has pledged to pause data center approvals in a state that is home to more than a quarter of planned U.S. data center capacity, CNBC reported.

A delay to Anthropic's IPO is now an "escalating risk" in the current environment, technology analyst Richard Windsor told The Telegraph. "A postponement would protect against near-term valuation risks and would be more likely if the correction sustains over a longer period," said Nalin Patel, an analyst at PitchBook, according to the paper.

Some investors want to see Anthropic's October results before assuming a cut. The company's annualized revenue run rate topped $80 billion at the end of October, and it had $146.6 billion in cash as of September 30, according to the Journal.

Traders have pushed back their expectations. On Polymarket, the odds that Anthropic lists by November 20 stood at 43% on Friday, while the chance of a listing by the end of the year was 71%. On Kalshi, where traders bet on when Anthropic will officially confirm its IPO, the odds of that happening before December 1 fell 15 points to 59%, while the chance of it happening before January 1 slipped to 68%. On Decrypt Predict, a prediction market developed by Decrypt's parent company, Dastan, traders also put the odds of an Anthropic IPO before 2027 at 71%.

The valuation now in question is already well below earlier expectations. In September, Decrypt reported that a leaked draft filing showed Anthropic was seeking a valuation of around $2 trillion.

Meanwhile, Dominick John, analyst at Zeus Research, said the decline in Nvidia per "signaled weakening near-term sentiment and increased bearish positioning."

"Deeply negative funding suggests short positioning may be becoming crowded, leaving room for a relief rebound if underlying U.S. equities stabilize," said John.

#5130. Zama spin-off Raycash launches confidential stablecoin payments and private yield on Ethereum

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Raycash says it has more than 75,000 people on its waitlist and is offering USDC payments and yield through Morpho.

Raycash, a stablecoin payments startup spun out of confidentiality protocol Zama (ZAMA), has launched on Ethereum mainnet, initially through a private beta.

The app is the first retail payment product built on the Zama Protocol, which uses Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) to keep onchain balances and transaction values encrypted, according to a statement shared exclusively with The Block. It enables users to hold dollars, send peer-to-peer payments, and earn yield on their balances while keeping amounts confidential. Raycash said more than 75,000 people joined a waitlist ahead of the launch, with access opening in stages during the private beta, followed by broader availability.

Zama launched its first confidential USDC yield vault in June with Morpho and Steakhouse Financial. The vault went from zero to $40 million in total value locked within seven weeks. Zama expanded that lineup to 16 confidential vaults on Sept. 15, and launched private swaps on Ethereum.

Raycash initially supports USDC at launch, with more stablecoins planned. The app supports deposits via bank transfer, card, or stablecoin, and withdrawals to bank accounts. Users can send, request, and receive payments instantly using payment links, and receive dollars as stablecoins from others. The project said it handles Ethereum network fees in the background, meaning users don't need to hold ETH in their wallets for gas fees.

Raycash said that while stablecoins are increasingly being used for saving and sending money, the nature of public blockchains means every account balance and transfer amount is visible to anyone with the address. Instead, Raycash allows users to wrap existing stablecoins 1:1 into ERC-7984 tokens, a confidential token standard built using Zama's FHE technology. Payments settle onchain as normal, but the amounts and balances stay encrypted, and the underlying stablecoins can be withdrawn through permissionless unwrapping, according to the project.

Raycash said its wallet is self-custodial and that the confidential tokens have no freeze or seize functions at the token layer. However, users can grant view access to specific parties, such as an accountant or auditor, without making their activity public.

Users can also earn yield privately on idle balances through DeFi platform Morpho, directly from the app, the project said, adding that a Raycard for private spending is also in development.

"People shouldn't have to choose between the benefits of stablecoins and keeping their financial information confidential," Raycash CEO and co-founder Valerio Leo said. "We don't ask anyone to trust a new stablecoin: we take the dollars people already use and make them private, and they can move back out whenever they want."

Starknet considers L1 transition - live with Eli Ben-Sasson

#5129. Russia finalizes crypto trading rules ahead of Nov. 17 rollout

The rules allow retail investors to trade BTC, ETH and USDT through regulated intermediaries, with Moscow Exchange planning to launch crypto trading in December.

Russia’s central bank has finalized rules allowing exchanges to offer Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and Tether USDt (USDT) to retail investors starting Nov. 17.

Under the rules, non-qualified investors can purchase up to 300,000 rubles (around $3,700) in cryptocurrency annually through each intermediary, including brokers, asset managers and crypto exchange providers. Qualified investors face no purchase limits, but all investors must pass a mandatory test before trading.

The directive builds on legislation signed by Russian President Vladimir Putin on Aug. 4 establishing a regulated cryptocurrency market under central bank oversight.

A week later, on Aug. 11, the Bank of Russia published its draft regulations, identifying BTC, ETH and USDT as eligible for public trading based on market capitalization, average daily trading volume and at least five years of price history on foreign exchanges.

The law took effect Sept. 1, and the Bank of Russia subsequently authorized Sberbank, the country’s largest bank, to offer crypto exchange services in October.

The regulatory developments come as Moscow Exchange, Russia’s largest securities exchange and a publicly traded company, prepares to launch cryptocurrency trading on Dec. 1, initially offering the three cryptocurrencies through pilot transactions.

While expanding access to cryptocurrencies through regulated platforms, Russia is also tightening restrictions on transactions outside those channels.

Direct peer-to-peer crypto trading will be prohibited starting July 1, 2027, while transfers from regulated digital accounts to self-custody wallets are already banned, according to the Bank of Russia. The country also maintains a prohibition on using cryptocurrency to pay for goods and services.
