#5006. In This Midcentury Nashville Home a Mosaic Rainbow-Hued Window Brings Color to the Earthy Interiors

Here’s one you may not have heard before: What do Serge Mouille sconces, a Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant, and Borge Mogensen dining chairs have in common? Hint, hint: They all live in the Nashville home of Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of antiques and vintage furniture store Eneby Home.

One might reasonably presume the home of antiques purveyors to be chock-full of one-of-a-kind finds from far-flung locales, and while that is certainly the case here, the husband-and-wife duo’s house otherwise expertly subverts expectations.

Built in 1970, the uber-quiet four-acre property sits at the top of a hill overlooking seemingly endless rolling valleys. It’s surrounded by native honeysuckle bushes and Tennessee Fieldstone, and the sun sets on the front side of the home at 6:37 p.m., says Doug, who dreamed of building what he describes as an “art gallery” for their furniture.

The Jenkinses kept the wall of original Tennessee Fieldstone—and the original copper fireplace—but added their own flair with vintage furnishings, like a La Mura sofa, Gianfranco Frattini for Cassina leather lounge chairs, a Charles Dudouyt credenza, and a 1960s marble fossil coffee table from Italy. “It’s super unique,” says Doug. “It doesn’t have a name behind it.”

Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of Eneby Home, with their daughters. They kept the original Tennessee Fieldstone on the wall and paired it with an antique marble dining table that’s “huge and heavy,” says Doug. “The guys hated bringing stuff to our house.” Even though the Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant is often seen in copper, the Jenkins were able to source one with a green finish, seen here. The chairs are Borge Mogensen; the 1960s geometric painting is from France.

A well-lit workspace features a vintage 1950s desk, an Italian cocoon lamp, and a Hans Wegner chair from Denmark.

“There’s not a lot of this type of house in Nashville,” says Greg Dutton, the founder and architect behind Columbus, Ohio-based and AD PRO Directory design firm Dutton Studio. “But we had to look past what was there—a mixed bag with a new era in each room—to create a backdrop for the work they do.”

Cue the blank slate. The couple bought the house as-is—black toilets, mustard-hued siding, and early 2000s kitchen included—and embarked on a “bigger than expected” renovation, says Doug, that translated to moving walls, updating floor plans, a new roof and flooring, and overall furnishings, of course.

With the resulting minimalist white walls and clean lines, how does one amass the amount of character evident here? “When you build in materials that have inherent imperfection, that naturally bakes in a lot of warmth,” says Dutton. Think locally sourced, character-grade white oak flooring throughout; imported reclaimed European bluestone that’s well over 100 years old in the primary bath; and an original copper fireplace in the living room.

“The mosaic supposedly came from a building in Bologna,” says Doug. “The morning sun comes up on this side” and ignites a fire of filtered light within the home.

“The island has a commercial look to it,” says Doug of the Vipp system. “It hit every box. It’s sick.” Dutton helped reconfigure the space’s floor plan to open the room to the dining area. The oven is by Miele.

A Guillerme et Chambron cabinet, sourced from Eneby Home, houses curiosities. The 1960s pendant light is Stilnovo.

The guest bathroom originally had a black toilet and smoked glass. Now it features vintage Serge Mouille sconces, of which Doug has “never seen another pair,” a 250-year-old antique Italian mirror from the 1800s, and a Josef Hoffman bench.

“Flaws make the house feel better, not worse,” says Dutton. “But creating something that looks effortless actually takes a lot of coordination. We wanted it to feel like it had just happened that way, and installing materials that feel like they’ve been there forever automatically gives the house permanence and ensures it’ll age gracefully over time.”

The push-and-pull between old and new, simple and full of character, clean and distressed is one the Jenkins family is familiar with. The balancing act makes the house interesting, says Doug, and the same inclination to lean on historic materials translates just as well to furnishings.

“Some of this stuff we’d been saving in hopes we’d one day be able to use it for our own house,” says Doug. One such example is a 1940s or ’50s-era, rainbow-hued mosaic window the couple bought five years ago and stored in a crate ever since. “We asked Greg to change the layout of the wall to make it work,” says Doug. The stone sculpture on display in front of the window is another; it’s 2,000 years old, from Rome.

An earthy palette in the primary bedroom lets the landscape through the windows do the talking. The sconces are by Biny; the oversized 1880 landscape painting is from France; the chairs are Borge Mogensen; the side table is Charles Dudouyt, and the floor lamp is La Borne.

“The fireplace is original, and we ran with it,” says Doug. “It’s New Mexico style and is super cool.” It’s paired with an abstract geometric art piece and a collection of vases from Denmark.

The stainless steel Vipp island in the kitchen, which the couple found in Denmark, is “the best thing in the house,” says Carina. “We moved from a house with a big pantry, and it was a challenge to figure out where everything would go. This island holds a lot.” Here, the family embraces the “European model” of compact living–albeit in 4,500 square feet. Dutton designed the black cabinetry wall as a backdrop for the island, which he calls a “piece of jewelry.”

Like any gallerist worth his salt, Doug spends five minutes each night turning off all of the ambient lighting in the house, like a pair of midcentury Biny sconces in the primary bedroom, an Orocolo lamp in the living room, and a 1970s Marika Carniti Bollea Nautilus lamp in the kitchen. Much of the light shines on a collection of artwork that’s so vast it’s often swapped out.

“We’ve already traded a few things,” says Doug. “If we find something we love, we want to use it. It’s what we do. It’s a perk, for sure.”

As is a home that’s forever. “Our girls want to come here when we’re old with their own kids,” says Carina. “They love it so much.”

The 1950s painting is from Denmark, the 1970s vase is from Sweden, the 1970s stool is Pierre Chapo, and the ceramic is from France. “We spend a lot of time in Denmark and like to mix Danish and Swedish,” says Doug. “We know a lot about that style of furniture.”

Dutton reworked the floor plan in the primary bathroom to set the vanity under a skylight. “We don’t use much overhead lighting,” says Carina.

#5002. Switching from GLP-1 Injections to Wegovy Pill Shows Additional Weight Loss of 4.1%

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

People with overweight or obesity who switch from an injectable GLP-1 medication to the Wegovy (semaglutide) pill may continue to lose weight, according to findings from the OCTANE study presented at the 2026 Meeting of the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) in Milan, Italy.

The study is among the first real-world analyses to examine weight loss and treatment experiences among people who switched from an injectable weight loss medication to oral semaglutide (Wegovy).

However, while real-world studies like this can provide useful insights into how treatments work in everyday life, these findings do not prove that switching to the Wegovy pill caused the weight loss.

The results have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

This was a retrospective cohort analysis evaluating weight change, treatment adherence, and patient satisfaction after people switched from injectable semaglutide (Wegovy) or tirzepatide (Zepbound) to oral semaglutide for weight management.

The study included a random sample of 194 adults with overweight or obesity and at least one obesity-related complication, such as hypertension, cardiovascular disease, obstructive sleep apnea, and dyslipidemia.

Participants had no history of diabetes or bariatric surgery and had an average baseline weight of 221.6 pounds (100.5 kg) and an average body mass index (BMI) of 34.5 kg/m2.

Participants switched from an injectable GLP-1 medication to oral semaglutide between January 5 and February 7, 2026. There was no requirement for how long they had used an injectable medication or how much weight they had lost before switching.

Participants were required to use oral semaglutide regularly for 3 months and self-report their weight within 30 days of starting treatment and again at the end of the study period.

After 3 months of taking oral semaglutide, participants had lost an average of 4.1% of their body weight, or 8.8 pounds.

At the 3-month mark, 2 in 5 participants (40.7%) had lost 5% or more of their body weight.

The proportion of participants with a BMI of 30 or greater also decreased by 21.6 percentage points, from 87.1% at baseline to 65.5% after 3 months.

Most participants (82.4%) reported at least one treatment improvement. Around 69% reported better-fitting clothes, and around 51% reported eating healthier.

Betul Hatipoglu, MD, Medical Director, Diabetes & Metabolic Center at University Hospitals, Cleveland, OH, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that, “These findings are encouraging because they suggest that some people can continue losing weight after switching to oral treatment.”

However, she also noted, “I would view this as promising early evidence supporting another treatment option, rather than proof that switching improves results.”

Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, noted that because the oral medication is absorbed through the gastrointestinal tract, less of it reaches the bloodstream compared to the injectable form.

However, oral semaglutide can still be effective for weight loss.

“While studies show that the injectable form is more efficacious, current findings demonstrate that the oral version is also effective for weight loss,” said Ali.

Some people may prefer the oral form for ease of use and transportation because it does not require refrigeration. However, it must be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, followed by a 30-minute wait before eating, Ali told Healthline.

“Both formulations can cause gastrointestinal side effects, including nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and constipation. Taking a pill does not eliminate those risks,” Hatipoglu said.

Hatipoglu also said factors to consider include how well your current treatment is working, side effects, your comfort with injections, your daily routine, cost, and insurance coverage.

She added that the decision should be made with a prescribing clinician, who can help plan the transition and monitor your response.

“Both options can be effective, so patients should discuss the risks and benefits of each with their provider. Ultimately, these medications are tools to support a healthier diet and lifestyle, and patients who make these fundamental changes achieve the best results,” said Ali.

#4998. Victorian Architecture Meets Mediterranean Charm in This Warm and Whimsical London Home

Interior designer Marta de la Rica describes herself as “a New Yorker by birth, French by adoption, and Spanish at heart.” That mix of cultures would prove to be an asset for one of her recent projects: the 5,382-square-foot four-story home of a family of four in London. A fine example of Victorian architecture, the building, located in the quiet residential neighborhood of Battersea and only steps away from Battersea Park, was built in 1879. “I already knew the clients, since I had previously designed their home in Bilbao, and I knew what kind of atmosphere the family wanted as well as what was particularly important to them about their home,” says de la Rica.

The walls of the stairwell are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks and the Clam Shell Large light fixtures are by Porta Romana. The floor is made of several different marbles: Negro Marquina, Perlado, and Rojo Alicante.

The young family is originally from Spain but had already been living in London for nearly 10 years when they decided to buy a single-family home there. De la Rica decided to design spaces for them with a British-Mediterranean look that reflects the character of the historic Victorian architecture and the demands of modern family life. “The family wanted an elegant and peaceful home that, above all, would be warm and cozy,” says de la Rica. “For us, that meant creating spaces with comfort, character, and personality.”

Two midcentury armchairs sit in front of a Louis XIV fireplace from 1700.

Light brown and rust tones dominate the living room’s color palette.

Since the clients are Spanish, the project had a particularly interesting cultural dimension for the designer, as she explains: “We wanted the house to feel personal to them while also remaining true to its London identity and the Victorian architecture. So the project became a dialogue between English interior design and a more relaxed, Mediterranean lifestyle,” says de la Rica.

De la Rica’s interiors combine English interior design and a relaxed lifestyle in this Victorian building where the sunroom doubles as a dining room.

Originally, the family had been looking for a home in a different part of London, but when they saw this house, they were immediately taken with it and changed their plans. The building’s structure was in very good condition; only the floor plan needed to be reconfigured. “Above all, we wanted to preserve the spacious rooms, the ceiling heights, the bay windows, and the staircase,” explains the designer.

A relaxed breakfast setting with chairs by Vintola Studio and Benjamin pendant lights from Felix Lighting,

Visually, all the rooms have an almost opulent feel, yet at the same time, each one reflects its particular purpose. The ground floor includes a study, a living room, a kitchen, af amily room, and a sunroom, which also serves as the dining room. The parents’ bedroom is located on the second floor. A separate children’s area with bedrooms and play areas was created on the top floor. “The sunroom is a big part of the character of the house. To build the basement, it had to be demolished and rebuilt. It was very important to us to preserve the extraordinary presence of this space and its connection to the rest of the house,” says de la Rica.

The designer made the transitions from one room to the next as natural as possible by ensuring that the materials and atmosphere flow seamlessly.

The living spaces flow into the garden (designed by Matthew Wilson) and the terraces in front of the family room and the sunroom provide direct access to the outdoors. The garden features a pool, an outdoor kitchen, and a spacious dining area, providing additional spaces for social gatherings. The interior is characterized by elegant craftsmanship, custom details, warm colors, and high-quality materials. These choices highlight the grandeur of the historic home while also creating a cozy atmosphere.

A 20th-century dresser serves as a nightstand in the primary bedroom; an onyx lamp sits next to a vase filled with flowers.

“I particularly like that the rooms all have their own character, yet there is a clear, unifying design that carries throughout the house,” says the designer. It was also important to de la Rica to emphasize the scale of the house without having it feel cold or ostentatious. “Even in the larger rooms, there’s a coziness. It was very important to us to achieve this balance,” she says.

In the guest bathroom, the walls are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks. Below the Negro Marquina marble sink by MDLR-Design, a curtain of Marly fabric by Colony.

The playroom showcases the Victorian architecture.

The children’s rooms are located under the mansard roof, which is typical of many Victorian houses in London. For the two youngest members of the family, the designer transformed the entire top floor with its sloped ceilings into colorful playrooms.

Amid dinosaurs and Mickey Mouse toys, the family’s son sleeps here. A Globe ceiling light hangs above the bed (Olofane).

De la Rica’s favorite parts of the house, however, are the living room and the dining room. For her, these are the areas where the home truly comes to life and where the design directly interacts with the family’s daily routine.

Above the large sofa in the family room, a Soren globe light from Pinch. The cream-colored Mayan Cuzco rug is from Bellbridge.

She particularly likes that the living room, dining room, and kitchen form a single, open space. “This part of the house allows for a very uncomplicated way of living,” she explains. “It’s very beautiful but not contrived or overly formal. It exudes coziness and spontaneity, and for me, that’s ultimately what makes a family home.”

For the walls in this bathroom, the designer chose Oli-03 Smooth by Clayworks, wall sconces by Vaughan, and a Calacatta Viola marble sink.

The wardrobe in the guest bedroom is upholstered with Ticking Stripe 1 Black fabric (Ian Mankin). The decorative Flax 03 tassels are from Samuel & Sons.

In the daughter’s bedroom, the rug is from Kvadrat. The closet doors are upholstered with fabrics from Yutes.

The Bert & May tiles in Garnet and Sand White were custom-made for this bathroom. The bathtub is Perlado marble.

This article originally appeared in the German edition of Architectural Digest.

#4995. BMW’s new iX5 looks like a big upgrade next to the old X5 [Images]

BMW’s new iX5 is hard to miss with its distinct Double-X headlights. Here’s how it stacks up against the outgoing X5, side by side.

The X5 is among BMW’s best-selling vehicles in the US. In its fifth generation, it will be available across five powertrains.

In the US, it will be offered in gas, plug-in hybrid (PHEV), and, for the first time, battery electric (EV) powertrains. Other markets will also see diesel and hydrogen fuel cell variants.

The gas-powered 2027 X5 40 xDrive is now available, starting at $72,100, but the electric iX5 won’t go on sale until November.

Ahead of its official launch next month, Bimmertoday posted several images of the new iX5 parked next to the outgoing X5 G05, giving us a good side-by-side comparison.

Although it still looks like an X5, the new iX5 stands apart with BMW’s Neue Klasse design. The new upright, slimmer BMW Kidney Grille replaces the old wide, chunkier grille design.

A post shared by bimmertoday (@bimmertoday)

You can also see BMW’s new “double-X” light signature, which combines the headlights, daytime running lights, side lights, and turn signals into one unit. The Double-X headlights come standard, but BMW said you can turn them off for simpler diagonal stripes.

The new BMW iX5 60 xDrive will go on sale in the US next month. Using a 144 kWh battery, BMW said the electric SUV will offer up to 435 miles of range.

Last week, BMW showcased the i5’s fast-charging capabilities for the first time during a media event in Southern California with IONNA and Alpitronic. During the event, the iX5 60 xDrive hit a peak of 464 kW, which BMW said enables it to add up to 170 miles of range in 10 minutes.

The iX5 is powered by a dual-motor all-wheel-drive powertrain, delivering a combined 578 hp (425 kW) and 593 lb-ft of torque. BMW says it can go from 0 to 60 mph in 4.4 seconds and tow up to 6,000 lbs.

Love this. If I were in the market for a new vehicle this would not just be on the list, it would be the list. With 450 miles of range, fast charging that adds 300 miles in 20 minutes, outstanding reviews for ride quality, cabin silence, L2++ ADAS, etc. it is the benchmark for EVs in America. Glad to see BMW bringing the A game and glad that someone is building a world class EV in America.

Inside, the iX5 adopts BMW’s Neue Klasse design, which includes a 17.9″ navigation touchscreen and the new BMW Panoramic Vision that stretches across the bottom of the windshield. Buyers can opt to add the optional 14.6″ passenger screen for a third display.

BMW’s new iX5 is 197.2″ long, 78.7″ wide, and 68.9″ tall, or about the size of the Porsche Cayenne Electric.

BMW will open orders for the new iX5 60 xDrive in November, starting at $79,800. It will be BMW’s first electric vehicle built in the US. Production is set to begin at its Plant Spartanburg in South Carolina by the end of the year.

#4994. Inside the Underground Mosques of Kazakhstan’s Surreal Mangystau Desert

Up until about 20 years ago, a faint trail up a steep hill through limestone-and-chalk cliffs was the only way to reach Beket-Ata, one of the sacred underground mosques—as they’re colloquially known—of Kazakhstan’s Mangystau desert. “There was no proper path then, but people made the difficult climb in the heat and wind,” my guide, Yerzhan Nagmanov, tells me as we embark on our way to the holy site—eased slightly by the stairs now carved into the stone. That arduous journey, the travelers believed, “was part of the pilgrimage.”

En route to Beket-Ata with Mangystau Tour 999, visitors walk a trail cutting through the steep hill.

The trail of stairs descending into the canyon en route to Beket-Ata.

As inhospitable as the roughly 64,000-square-mile desert may seem, it has attracted scores of people over the years arriving to see Beket-Ata and the four other sacred complexes scattered across its expanse: Shopan-Ata, Karaman-Ata, Shakpak-Ata and Sultan-Epe. Traveling between the five holy sites, the distinction between architecture and geology evaporates into the desert heat: Walls, ceilings, and floors are all variations of the rock from which the mosques were excavated. For pilgrims, the journey to these sacred sites is an act of devotion, where people come to honor revered Sufi masters, pray for blessings, and seek spiritual guidance in times of difficulty.

The mosques are hundreds of years old, collectively dating between the 12th and 18th centuries, but they’re new to the UNESCO World Heritage List as of this summer. The “Rock Mosques and Associated Sacred Sites of Mangystau” were added to the esteemed collection of landmarks in July.

Each mosque is associated with a different spiritual master within Sufism, the mystical branch of Islam. The five Sufi masters’ influence shaped the region’s religious landscape, and then the landscape itself, as all of the mosques—crafted in a rock-cut style that grew out of nomadic building traditions—seem to grow directly from the desert terrain; they’re carved into caves, tucked along the contours of cliffs and hillsides, and tunneled below the ground.

After three weeks travelling through Kazakhstan’s cities, mountains, and pine forests, I spent my final week in Mangystau, a landscape so different from anything I’d seen so far in the country that it felt like a whole world unto itself. Having recently visited Astana’s Grand Mosque, with its blue domes, soaring minarets, and gleaming expanses of marble, I wondered whether Mangystau’s rock-cut shrines might fall flat by comparison. For me, however, their lack of splashy grandeur is precisely what made them so compelling.

A stately arch welcomes travelers to the steps leading to Beket-Ata.

While I am not Muslim, I was fascinated by how the Sufi masters traversed this unforgiving terrain along with their families. Some walked hundreds of kilometers to establish places of worship far from the comforts of the world, spending years in prayer and contemplation before eventually being buried here. I was drawn to the way the rock mosques embody a humble yet stunning expression of faith shaped by the austere landscape, where instead of gilded shrines or massive palaces, devotion found a more organic form. Through guided 4x4 tours with Red Maya Travel and Mangystau Tour 999, I visited all five.

A necropolis inside the Shopan-Ata complex.

My first stop on the tour is Shopan-Ata, named after the 12th-century Sufi teacher. The complex consists of a vast necropolis (a large burial ground with tombs of religious figures and generations of pilgrims) of more than 2,000 monuments, including domed mausoleums and kulpytas, which are ornately carved stone steles traditionally used as Kazakh gravestones. The 12-chamber mosque is carved into the rock, and a shaft of daylight is cast onto two sacred wooden poles below. According to a local tradition, pilgrims recite a prayer and circle the poles three times counterclockwise, which is believed to help the spirits hear them. Visitors also tie strips of white cloth around the poles, the knot symbolically binding a prayer to the sacred site. These practices likely come from Shamanic and Tengrian traditions of tying strips of cloth to sacred trees and mountain passes to honor nature spirits––customs that became intertwined with local forms of Muslim saint veneration over time.

Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite; these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

Domed mausoleums at the Shopan-Ata necropolis.

The entrance to Shopan-Ata, shaded by a mulberry tree. These mosques were built around oases that sustained life in the harsh desert climate.

Legend holds that centuries later, Shopan-Ata appeared to Beket-Ata in a dream, offering spiritual guidance, making this shrine an important stop before continuing to Beket-Ata. Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite, its water an incongruous presence in a landscape this dry––a reminder that these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

The sacred wooden pole, around which visitors tie white cloth to seek blessings.

The final few stairs leading up to the Beket-Ata shrine.

Just over an hour away, Beket-Ata is perhaps the best-known of the five mosques. Founded by the 18th-century Sufi teacher Beket Myrzagululy, the shrine lies in a remote canyon about 170 miles from Aktau, the Caspian-side gateway to Mangystau. The steep descent into the canyon is a reminder that the effort needed to reach these shrines has always been a part of the devotion.

The structure is carved into the pale rock, its interconnected chambers following the curves of the cliff. Narrow openings in the ceilings pull shafts of daylight into the cool, dim rooms, where rugs are spread across the stone floor.

Communal dining is integral to Kazakh culture.

An imam is usually on hand to receive visitors and share information about the site. As we settle on the carpets, he details each of the chambers, one of which contains Beket-Ata’s grave, and another is associated with his sister. The remaining rooms are used for prayer, with separate spaces for men and women.

My pilgrimage continues into the dining hall, where a long, continually replenished table runs through the room, with pilgrims sitting on either side and eating together. The mosque provides some freshly cooked meat free of charge, while visitors leave bread, sweets, tea, yogurt, jam and other provisions for those who arrive after them—an especially vivid expression of Central Asian hospitality.

Next we drive across remote country to the Tyub-Karagan Peninsula for Shakpak-Ata, whose cruciform mosque is carved into a massive chalk cliff. Its porous white surface, which looks almost like honeycomb against the desert, was shaped millions of years ago when this part of Mangystau lay beneath the ancient Tethys Sea. Inside, drawings and inscriptions in Arabic, Persian, and Turkic—some dating back to the 18th century—offer a tangible record of the pilgrims who traveled here from across Central Asia, long before air-conditioned 4x4s and Starlink internet made traversing this unforgiving landscape simpler.

A honeycomb-like pattern along the rock face of Shakpak-Ata.

Slightly west, Sultan-Epe lies beside a deep canyon, where a stream threads through thickets of mulberry, buckthorn, blackberry, and wild herbs—another improbable pocket of green in one of Kazakhstan’s driest regions. Sultan-Epe is revered as a protector of seafarers and fishermen—a fitting association for a shrine overlooking the Caspian. According to local tradition, Sultan-Epe was the son of Hakim-Ata, a renowned Sufi poet and disciple of Khoja Ahmed Yasawi, which connects the shrine to the spiritual tradition associated with Shopan-Ata and, through his later spiritual mentor, Beket-Ata.

The stacked-stone entrance to Sultan-Epe.

The final stop on the circuit is Karaman-Ata, where a seven-hectare necropolis stretches across a low, open plot of desert dotted with funerary monuments, some more than 1,000 years old. The mosque is carved into the slope of a small marl hill, and as you descend into the rock, the temperature drops noticeably from the heat outside. A narrow passage leads to a circular room believed to be associated with Sufi rituals including devotional whirling, a type of worship that is expressed through physical movement.

Karaman-Ata, the largest of the five rock mosques of Kazhakstan’s Mangystau desert.

Reaching each site still means long drives across remote terrain, with limited to no mobile coverage or fuel services–making a sturdy vehicle and a trusted local guide essential. But after days spent traveling the region, these places begin to alter your sense of the desert. Around a well, beneath a mulberry tree, or at a table crowded with food and strangers, the landscape that seemed so stark and inhospitable reveals an unexpected generosity, preserved in the rituals of those who continue to make the journey here.

#4992. Tesla appears to be preparing to sell US-built Model 3s in Canada again

Tesla Canada has updated the Model 3 Premium Rear-Wheel Drive on its website with 584 km of range and a 0-100 km/h time of 5.2 seconds. Those are the exact specs of the Fremont-built Long Range RWD that Tesla sold in Canada two years ago.

The Shanghai-built version, the $39,490 CAD car Canadians haven’t been able to order since early October, is rated at 463 km and 6.2 seconds. The new numbers point to a different car from a different factory.

Canadian Tesla owner RJ was first to spot the change on X:

Tesla Canada 🇨🇦 has updated Model 3 Premium RWD range and 0-100 specs.New specs:584 km range (previously 463 km)0-100 in 5.2 seconds (previously 6.2 seconds)Note: Premium Model 3 RWD orders are currently paused because the Government of Canada states Tesla has exhausted its… pic.twitter.com/PeWP15w2RS

The new figures are in the “Model 3 Premium Specs” section of Tesla’s Canadian Model 3 page, under the Rear-Wheel Drive tab, along with a curb weight of 1,745 kg. The page’s marketing copy now also promises “up to 584 km (EPA est.) on a single charge.”

Tesla hasn’t touched its Canadian comparison page, though. As of this afternoon, it still lists the Model 3 Premium RWD at 463 km, 6.2 seconds and $39,490.

In October 2024, Tesla Canada listed the Model 3 Long Range RWD at 584 km and 5.2 seconds, starting at $54,990 and eligible for the federal iZEV rebate. That car came from Fremont.

They also line up with the Model 3 Premium RWD Tesla sells in the US today. Tesla’s US website rates it at 363 miles of EPA range, which converts to 584 km, with 0-60 mph in 4.9 seconds. It starts at $42,490 USD.

There’s one other candidate. Giga Shanghai builds a Model 3 Long Range RWD for the Chinese market with a 78.4 kWh pack, 830 km of CLTC range and the same 5.2-second 0-100 km/h time.

But a Shanghai-built car runs into the same quota problem that paused Model 3 orders in Canada earlier this month. Tesla’s website says orders will resume “if Govt of Canada issues more quota, expected March 2027.” Meanwhile, the federal tracker showed 33,069 of the period’s permits still unused as of October 9.

So if Tesla wants to sell a long-range Model 3 in Canada before March, it has to come from Fremont.

And that’s where it gets expensive. Canada’s 25% counter-tariffs on US-made vehicles, in place since April 2025, still apply. The Department of Finance’s counter-tariff page, updated October 9, says the measures against US autos “continue to apply.”

Those tariffs are what pushed Tesla out of US-built Model 3s in the first place. When they hit, Tesla raised the Model 3 Long Range AWD by $11,000 to $79,990 and dropped the $55,990 RWD entirely. In March, it shipped its remaining US-built Model 3 inventory out of Canada ahead of the switch to Shanghai, which led to the record-low $39,490 Model 3 Premium RWD in May.

If Tesla applies a similar markup to a Fremont-built Premium RWD, it would land somewhere around $65,000 to $67,000. That’s well above the $50,000 transaction cap of Canada’s new $5,000 EV Affordability Program, even though US-built cars otherwise qualify through CUSMA (Chinese-built ones don’t).

Right now, the only Model 3 you can order in Canada is the Performance, which Tesla priced at $74,990 when it cut its price in May.

Do Canadians really want to buy from a guy helping the trade war against Canada? I hope not.

Tesla hasn’t announced anything, and the configurator hasn’t changed. But Tesla doesn’t usually rewrite a spec sheet for a car it isn’t planning to sell, and these numbers match a car it has sold in Canada before.

The most logical explanation is a stopgap. Tesla had a real hit with the $39,490 Model 3, ran through its quota in about four months, and is now stuck selling a $75,000 Performance until March. A Fremont-built Premium RWD would give buyers a long-range Model 3 at something less painful than that.

The problem is that it’d be a tough sell. I think Canadian buyers are more likely to wait and see if Tesla gets more quotas next and year. At the same time, they will wait to see what the Chinese automakers have to offer next year.

#4990. Detached Nails More Than 4 Times Higher Among GLP-1 Users, Study Finds

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

GLP-1 users have reported various unusual side effects like “Ozempic feet,” “Ozempic butt,” and “Ozempic breasts.” These changes have been largely tied to rapid weight loss.

Now, fingernails have entered the conversation. Nails detaching from the nail bed or changing color may be more common among people taking GLP-1 drugs, according to a new study. In the study, which researchers presented in October at the European Academy of Dermatology and Venereology (EADV) Congress 2026 in Vienna, people taking GLP-1 drugs such as semaglutide — Ozempic, Wegovy — and tirzepatide — Mounjaro, Zepbound — reported nail detachment and nail discoloration more often than people who didn’t take the drugs.

When the researchers counted only nail changes that started after treatment began, the link between GLP-1 use and nail detachment nearly disappeared, study author Charles Taïeb, MD, PhD, told Healthline.

“This is why we insist that a substantial part of the nail excess reflects the patients’ overall health rather than the drug itself, while a modest drug-specific effect remains plausible,” Taïeb said.Earlier research has also linked GLP-1 use to hair loss. In the new study, the researchers explained why they examined nails, noting that nails and hair both grow from cells that divide quickly.

However, the researchers cautioned in a press release that the study can’t establish that the drugs caused the nail changes. Both the nail study and a companion hair study captured self-reported symptoms at a single point in time, a design that can identify associations only, they said.

For the study, researchers examined a representative sample of people in France, the United States, Brazil, and Mexico.

Participants included 575 people taking GLP-1 drugs and 1,329 people with type 2 diabetes, obesity, or both conditions, who had never taken GLP-1 drugs.

The average treatment duration was 18.5 months, and researchers tracked weight loss over three months.

They also looked separately at people who had lost weight, to help tell whether the drugs or the weight loss explained the changes. However, the abstract didn’t specify which GLP-1 drugs participants took.

The researchers have submitted the findings to peer-reviewed dermatology journals, where they are under review, Taïeb told Healthline. Here’s what they found.

According to the American Academy of Dermatology (AAD), nail detachment, or onycholysis, occurs when a nail lifts, and the lifted area often appears white.

Taïeb told Healthline that the study asked about “nail detachment” because patients, not doctors, filled out the questionnaire. Nail detachment was the strongest signal among the five nail changes the researchers asked about, Taïeb said.

According to the abstract, 39% of GLP-1 users reported nail separation or detachment (onycholysis), compared with 9% of nonusers.

Nail discoloration is a change in a nail’s usual color, such as white, yellow, or greenish-black, according to the AAD.According to the abstract, 46% of GLP-1 users reported discoloration, compared with 17% of nonusers. Ridges or grooves and fragile nails were also more common among GLP-1 users.

Mir B. Ali, MD, a bariatric surgeon and medical director of the MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Los Angeles, said that while he prescribes GLP-1s, nail detachment hasn’t come up in his practice. Ali wasn’t involved in the research.“I haven’t had anybody complain of that. It’s not something we really see,” he told Healthline. He said his patients more often mention brittle nails or minor changes in nail color.

Over a three-month period, 76% of GLP-1 users reported weight loss, compared with 47% of nonusers, according to the abstract.Weight loss alone didn’t appear to explain the nail findings, the researchers said in the press release. The differences between users and nonusers held up when they looked only at people who had lost weight.The researchers also cautioned in the press release that the study can’t show the drugs caused the changes. They noted that about half of GLP-1 users had nail problems before starting treatment.

Adjusting for skin conditions and nutritional deficiencies weakened some of the associations, they said, though nail detachment and discoloration stayed about two to three times as common among users.Those adjustments relied on conditions participants reported, including iron, vitamin B12, zinc, and vitamin D deficiencies, as well as psoriasis, eczema, and other skin conditions, Taïeb said.“GLP-1 medications may affect the nails indirectly rather than directly,” said Tanya Evans, MD, a board certified dermatologist and medical director of the Skin Cancer Program at the Melanoma Clinic at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, who wasn’t involved in the research.Because the drugs reduce appetite, some people may eat less protein, iron, and zinc, which can contribute to brittle nails, discoloration, or nail detachment, Evans told Healthline. Rapid weight loss may also temporarily disrupt nail growth, much as it can trigger hair shedding, she said.Ali said the total amount of weight a person loses plays a bigger role in hair and nail changes than the rate at which they lose it.

The U.S. labels for Wegovy and Zepbound list hair loss as a side effect reported in clinical trials.

The labels for the Ozempic, both injectable and pill form, as well as Mounjaro, list hair loss as a side effect, based on reports received after the drugs entered the market. Those reports don’t establish how often it happens or whether the drugs caused it.

As such, researchers have been examining GLP-1-related hair loss.

A companion study of the same participants found hair and scalp changes were also more common among GLP-1 users, according to that study’s abstract, also provided to Healthline:

Scalp redness was about three times as common among users, and most users with hair loss also had scalp symptoms such as itching, redness, or scaling.Scalp inflammation may play a role in hair changes among GLP-1 users, in addition to the shedding that often follows rapid weight loss, one of the study’s authors stated in the news release.“This raises the possibility that inflammation may be contributing in some patients, rather than the problem being solely the classic temporary hair shedding associated with rapid weight loss,” Evans said.Experts have told Healthline that hair loss on GLP-1 drugs is often temporary and tied to weight loss.“Hair loss, we see commonly, not only in patients who are on GLP-1 medications, but also in our surgical weight loss patients,” Ali said.

“When you lose weight, especially in women, it releases hormones. You’re getting metabolic changes, and all these things can lead to hair loss and some changes in the nails.” Ali said he tells patients that “as the weight stabilizes at a healthy weight, the hormones stabilize and the hair tends to come back to where it was before.”

Nail detachment and discoloration don’t appear as adverse reactions in the current U.S. prescribing information for Ozempic, Wegovy, Mounjaro, or Zepbound.

Ali said the best thing patients can do is get the right nutrition, including enough protein. He said he also advises patients to take a multivitamin along with B-complex, vitamin D, and calcium.

“I would not recommend stopping the GLP-1 medication simply because nail detachment occurs,” Evans said. She advised people to tell their doctor about any new changes in hair, scalp, or nails.Treatment for nail detachment or discoloration depends on the cause, Evans said. A dermatologist can check for an infection, an inflammatory nail disorder, a nutritional problem, an injury, or another explanation, she said.A detached nail recovers as a new nail grows out from its base, Evans said. That takes about 4 to 6 months for a fingernail and 9 to 12 months for a toenail.

Lastly, the AAD recommends having a board certified dermatologist examine any nail that’s lifting.

The AAD also recommends seeing a dermatologist if skin, hair, or nail side effects don’t improve, as they can help determine whether symptoms are due to the medication or another condition.“The important message is don’t panic, but don’t ignore persistent nail changes either,” Evans said.

#4988. The 2027 Kia EV6 is over $8,000 more than the new EV3

Kia announced that 2027 EV6 prices start at about $38,000, or $8,000 more than the new EV3.

The 2027 Kia EV6 arrives as a carryover model from last year. After reducing prices on the 2026 model year by $5,000 just a few months ago, the 2027 EV6 remains the same price across all trims.

The base EV6 Light standard-range RWD model starts at $37,900, not including the $1,545 destination fee, which is the same as last year.

Powered by a 63 kWh battery, the standard-range variant offers up to 237 miles of range. Upgrading to the EV6 Light long range RWD model costs an extra $3,300, starting at $41,200, but delivers up to 319 miles of range using a larger 84 kWh battery.

The 84 kWh battery offers up to 319 miles of range in RWD variants, while AWD EV6 models get up to 295 miles. The top-spec EV6 GT-Line AWD has up to 270 miles of range.

Kia said that with the EV6 MSRP unchanged and the launch of the new EV3, it’s “making EVs more attainable for those who embrace the fully electric lifestyle.”

The 2027 Kia EV3 starts at just $29,890, or about $8,000 less than the EV6. Kia’s entry-level electric SUV is available in five trims: Light, Wind, Land, GT-Line, and GT.

The lowest-priced EV3 Light FWD model uses a 58.3 kWh battery and delivers an EPA-estimated range of up to 221 miles.

Higher-priced trims, including the Wind, Land, GT-Line, and GT, use a larger 81.4 kWh battery that delivers up to 321 miles of range on a single charge. Like the EV6, the EV3 is also available with front-wheel-drive and all-wheel-drive powertrain options.

Inside, both the EV6 and EV3 feature Kia’s ccNC infotainment system. The setup includes nearly 30″ of screen space in a curved panoramic display, including dual 12.3″ instrument cluster and central navigation screens, plus a 5″ climate control touchscreen.

Both also include a native NACS port so that you can access Tesla Superchargers without a separate adapter.

However, the EV6 uses an 800V architecture, enabling faster charging than the EV3, which uses a 400V system.

I've had four EVs and the 800v charging system on my 2025 Ioniq 5 is the biggest improvement I have experienced in any EV. My road trip stops are now about 20 minutes to go from 15% to 85% at fast chargers. To me, this is a game changer. When people talk about range anxiety, they are really talking about long recharging times and with these speeds, you'll be back on the road in the time it takes to hit the restroom and grab a bit to eat.

The EV6 can charge from 10% to 80% in about 18 minutes, whereas the EV3 takes about 30 minutes.

The EV6 is also larger and less boxy than the EV3, measuring 184.8″ long, 74″ wide, and 60.8″ tall, with a 114.2″ wheelbase. In comparison, the EV3 is 169.3″ long, 72.8″ wide, and 62″ tall, with a 105.5″ wheelbase.

Which one would you drive? The new EV3, the EV6, or would you go with Kia’s three-row EV9? Let us know in the comments.

With the new 2027 models arriving, Kia is offering a $10,000 customer cash discount on the 2026 EV9, $5,000 on the 2026 EV6, and $1,500 on the 2027 EV3 to clear inventory. You can use the links below to see what’s available near you.

#4987. 9 Best Desks for Small Spaces That Offer Savvy Organization (2026)

A desk in a small apartment rarely gets a room of its own. More often it shares a wall with the bed, borrows a corner of the living room, or has to hide somewhere when guests come over. This means the best desks for small spaces really earn their footprint. We’ve rounded up nine that do just that, including a wall desk that puts vertical space to work, a folding desk that collapses to under three inches, and several other petite options that hold their own as furniture, once the laptop is stowed.

Each pick is sized for a compact home office, a bedroom work setup, or an unclaimed nook, and we’ve noted dimensions, materials, and assembly for all of them. If you have a little more room to work with, many have bigger siblings in the same collection.

If you want a standing desk that doesn’t look like office equipment, turn to Article’s Torme. It adjusts its height with a manual lift mechanism hidden in the top, so you can switch between sitting and standing without a motor or a control panel. And while there are no drawers or additional shelves, a few divided compartments are within the desk’s body to keep your daily essentials close by.

For those interested in a work surface that can double as a vanity or living room sideboard, the Sloane Desk from Castlery is up to the task. Elevated by fluted panels and a duo of discreet storage drawers, its look is polished enough to nimbly complement non-office settings. At just over 47 inches wide, it also has room to work as a small computer desk with a monitor, and the drawers keep chargers and paperwork out of sight when it’s off duty.

This Scandi-style desk is all about taking up vertical space—one of our favorite space-saving hacks. Blending metal and wood in one streamlined design, it provides a top shelf for vertical storage and plenty of wall space to add artwork and decor. Choose between a black or white metal frame to match your home’s mood.

A treat for the eyes—and a space-saving solution for any small home—this compact wood desk pairs its clean, design-forward aesthetic with discreet, functional details: a drawer for extra storage, a hidden cubby for cable management, and a fully finished back that allows it to be displayed anywhere in the room. With solid wood legs and slats, it’s a sturdy home office addition that’s both handsome and heirloom-worthy. At $2,495, it’s the priciest pick here. But it’s also the most likely to outlast the apartment it was bought for.

This electric standing desk changes desk height at the touch of a button. At 34 inches wide, it’s the narrowest standing desk on this list. Available in three sizes and color combinations, its ice-blue finish provides a welcome break from ho-hum neutrals—and, if you’d like, you can order a desk chair in the exact same color for a sleek, monochrome setup.

The ultimate small-space-friendly desk is one that folds away when not in use. The Wohomo folding desk collapses to under three inches, so it can slide behind a door or into a closet between workdays. This Amazon option boasts thousands of positive reviews touting its slim profile, and it’s light enough for one person to move on their own. Available in three sizes (31 inches, 39 inches, and 47 inches), you’re able to choose how much workspace you’ll need. This desk is also equipped with a side hook ideal for keeping your headphones, work bag, or jacket off the ground.

When AD featured Emma Chamberlain’s Los Angeles home in 2022, she was already learning the ropes of decorating with the interiors firm Proem. She has since followed that project with a collection for West Elm. The house taught her to weigh function as heavily as looks. “If something looks great but feels bad, it doesn’t make the cut,” she previously told us. The 36-inch mini desk, shown here in dark walnut, is narrow enough for a bedroom corner or the end of a living room, and at $499 it lands in the middle of this list price-wise. If you have the wall for it, the collection also includes a 48-inch storage desk at $899.

What Are the Best Types of Desks for Small Spaces?

For most small spaces, the best options are wall-mounted desks and floating desks, which keep the floor clear and put vertical space to use. Folding desks are another great option since they’re easy to stow away when you’re done for the day. If you have an empty corner space, a corner desk or a small L-shaped desk can claim square footage that otherwise goes unused. A secretary desk, which closes over your work, makes sense if your home office is also your living room.

How Do I Choose the Right Small Computer Desk for My Needs?

The right computer desk for you will depend on your priorities. If you have multiple monitors, choose a desk with a longer shape and a cord organizer to ensure you have enough surface area. If you work on just a laptop, consider a portable C-shaped desk that can double as a side table when not in use. If your desk is too shallow for a keyboard and a screen, a keyboard tray mounted underneath buys you room without a bigger footprint.

While most sitting desks are between 27 and 30 inches tall, your height will play a factor in ergonomics and how comfortable you’ll feel sitting for long periods of time. For example, if you’re taller, you may find yourself slouching to type. For those who are shorter, your neck could be placed at an odd angle for viewing your laptop or monitor screen. If you’d rather keep the desk you already have, a standing desk converter sits on top of it and gives you an adjustable height without adding another piece of furniture.

Branch, a desk and office-chair company that is focused on ergonomics, recommends that the best desk height maintains a 90-degree angle at your elbows when your arms rest on the desk (a.k.a. while you’re typing). Your desk chair will also affect your comfort and ergonomic posture. You can shop our tested and researched picks in our office chairs guide.

What Are the Most Space-Efficient Desks for Small Apartments?

Vertical wall-mounted desks, foldable desks (basically the Murphy bed of workspaces), and C-shaped portable desks are going to be the most space-efficient options for small apartments. Foldable desks can easily be tucked away when not in use, while C-shaped tables can also function as a drinks table after working hours. If you need a space-saving desk with storage, look for vertical storage above the work surface rather than drawers below it, which keeps the desk’s footprint small.

Where Should I Put a Desk in a Small Apartment?

Start with the power outlets. A desk within reach of a wall plug saves you from running extension cords across the room, and it gives you an obvious place for a phone and laptop charging station. After that, look for natural light and a wall or corner that isn’t already doing another job.

#4985. Lectric launches XP Lite3 e-bike, the new best low-cost model in the US

Lectric eBikes, the largest electric bike company in the US by just about any metric you can measure, has just unveiled its latest model: the Lectric XP Lite3. And with a starting price of just $799, the new lightweight folding electric bike is continuing the company’s tradition of packing surprisingly impressive features into remarkably affordable e-bikes.

The XP Lite2 had been widely considered the best bang-for-your-buck option in the ultra-budget category, offering an affordable yet punchy Class 2 e-bike for a song, despite still coming from a reputable US-based company with strong reputation for warranty and support.

Now, the third-generation model builds on what has become one of the most successful budget e-bike platforms in the country, bringing a long list of upgrades designed to make the bike easier to ride, fold, transport, and live with.

And perhaps most importantly, Lectric has managed to keep that familiar $799 starting price that debuted on the original XP Lite1 all the way back in 2022.

The new Lectric XP Lite3 weighs just 49 lb (22.2 kg), making it one of the lighter options in Lectric’s extensive lineup of folding electric bikes. That’s still no featherweight, partly due to Lectric’s insistence on not neutering the bike’s performance, but it’s still a solid 10-20 lb lighter than the average direct-to-consumer e-bike these days.

The bike retains the compact folding design that made its predecessors popular with everyone from college students to RV owners, but introduces a redesigned frame with improved ergonomics and a reinforced folding latch borrowed from the company’s flagship XP4 model.

New quick-release pedals further reduce the bike’s storage footprint, making it easier to squeeze into a car trunk, apartment closet, or RV storage compartment.

Despite its compact dimensions, the XP Lite3 can support a maximum payload of 275 lb (125 kg) and accommodates riders ranging from 4’8” to 6’2”.

The bike reaches speeds of up to 20 mph (32 km/h) and offers up to 40 miles (64 km) or 80 miles (128 km) of range in Eco mode, though expect significantly less range when using higher-power pedal assist or throttle-only riding. The range discrepancy comes from two different battery offerings. The base model features a 374 Wh battery, while the long-range version has nearly twice the capacity at 672 Wh.

Power comes from Lectric’s Stealth M24 motor, which is rated at 300W continuous but over 800 peak watts. The motor combines with Lectric’s pedal assist selector to offer five levels of pedal assist that are personalizable, letting riders dial in exactly how much boost they’d like on their ride. Riders also get a twist throttle, a more comfortable style of throttle enabled by the lack of shifters crowding the bars on this single-speed e-bike.

Several other upgrades help the XP Lite3 punch above its weight class, including integrated rear turn signals, a bright 3.5-inch full-color display brought over from other recently launched models in the Lectric lineup, and a redesigned LevelUp Lite rear rack with new mounting possibilities.

Stopping power comes from Star Union 602 hydraulic disc brakes with thicker 2.3 mm rotors, another welcome feature on a bike at this price point.

Lectric is also offering a Gates Carbon Belt Drive version of the XP Lite3, giving riders a cleaner, quieter, and virtually maintenance-free alternative to a traditional chain drivetrain. That Gates belt drive model fetches a $100 premium, but is likely worth it for those who value the cleaner, maintenance-free alternative that is likely to outlast the bike itself.

The chain-drive version comes in Arctic White, Blue Mist, Matte Grey, and Matte Lavender, while the belt-drive model is offered in JW Black and Ballerina Pink.

It’s difficult to overstate just how much Lectric has changed the American electric bicycle industry.

The Phoenix, Arizona-based company has grown into the country’s largest e-bike brand by both sales volume and the total number of e-bikes on the road. In doing so, it has become one of the most recognizable names in affordable electric transportation.

But Lectric didn’t get there by simply building cheap electric bikes.

Instead, the company helped revolutionize what consumers should expect from a budget-friendly e-bike, combining capable hardware, practical designs, and aggressive pricing with something that has historically been missing from much of the low-cost e-bike market: a real American company standing behind the product.

While countless inexpensive e-bikes sold online come from little-known overseas brands with questionable long-term support, Lectric has built its reputation around US-based customer service, accessible replacement parts, and a company that riders can actually reach when something goes wrong.

That’s a big part of what has made the XP Lite series so important.

Since its introduction, the XP Lite series has arguably set the standard for what an entry-level electric bike should be. It isn’t necessarily the most powerful, fastest, or fanciest e-bike on the road, but it delivers the essentials at a price that opens electric cycling to an entirely new audience.

And importantly, it does so without requiring riders to gamble on an unknown brand.

The XP Lite3 continues that formula, adding features like hydraulic disc brakes, integrated turn signals, and a color display that would have been difficult to imagine on a $799 e-bike just a few years ago.

I’ve been following Lectric since the company’s earliest days, and it’s been fascinating to watch how the brand has consistently raised the bar for affordable electric bikes. I rode one of Lectric’s first 10 bikes off their nascent production line all the way back in 2019, and I think they’d agree that few people outside the company know the brand better than I do. So I can say that, without a doubt, this is one of the company’s most important launches to date because of how it revolutionizes how much bike someone can get without stretching their budget farther than they have to. And at a time when inflation is still rising unchecked and fuel prices are through the roof, this is what the industry needs more than ever.

Keep in mind that the $799 price tag for the XP Lite3 announced today is the same price that the original XP Lite carried back in 2022, despite that value ballooning to $939 in 2026 dollars due to rising inflation.

What impresses me most isn’t necessarily any single feature on the new XP Lite3. It’s the combination of all those features at a price that remains accessible to so many riders.

Hydraulic brakes, a color display, integrated turn signals, a refined folding mechanism, and even the option for a Gates belt drive? That’s quite a lot of bike for the money. And remember, the performance is still there. This is still a 48V e-bike with a legit 20 mph (32 km/h) top speed on throttle and pedal assist.

Sure, there are lighter e-bikes out there, and there are certainly more premium models with fancier components. But that’s not really the point of the XP Lite.

The point is to make electric biking accessible without making it feel like a compromise at every turn.

And when you consider that you’re getting all of this from the biggest e-bike company in the country, complete with US-based support and a proven track record, it’s hard to argue with the value proposition.

The original XP Lite helped establish what a good affordable electric bike could be. The XP Lite3 looks ready to move that benchmark forward once again.

And on a personal note, not only do I want my own Lectric XP Lite3 now, but I think my wife’s new e-bike will soon be an XP Lite3 in Ballerina Pink with a Gates belt drive. Remember, “Happy wife, happy life!”

#4983. Pain Prevalence Higher in Women, Increases Most Rapidly Before 55

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Women experience a higher prevalence of pain than men across different areas of the body, according to a large global study published October 5 in Nature Medicine.

The analysis, which included more than 6 million people, also found that pain prevalence generally increased with age, with the most rapid increases occurring before age 55.

The findings come as lower back pain, neck pain, and osteoarthritis — all common causes of pain — are projected to become more prevalent globally by 2050.

Pain, especially lower back pain, is the leading cause of disability worldwide. However, researchers have lacked a standardized way to examine how self-reported pain varies across the life span and different regions of the world.

This new study is one of the largest efforts to date to examine self-reported pain using a standardized approach.

The researchers analyzed data from 6,125,459 individuals spanning 118 countries from 1990 to 2025. Participants ranged in age from 5 to more than 100, and 55% were female.

They used self-reported data on pain across 11 body sites:

The researchers found that women consistently reported a higher prevalence of pain across all 11 sites than men.

The largest differences between women and men were in facial pain, headache, and stomach or abdominal pain.

Pain prevalence also increased most rapidly before age 55, especially at musculoskeletal sites such as the hips, back, and knees.

The researchers found that the prevalence of pain varied considerably by body region and differed by age.

Back pain was the most commonly reported, affecting 40% of participants, while facial pain was the least common at 2%.

Neel Anand, MD, a board certified orthopedic spine surgeon and director of the Cedars-Sinai Spine Center in Los Angeles, who wasn’t involved in the study, explained some factors that may contribute to the high prevalence of back pain.

“Our modern lifestyles involve more prolonged sitting, less physical activity, and more time spent looking down at phones, tablets, and computers, all of which can contribute to poor posture and increased stress on the spine,” he said.

“As we get older, the discs between our vertebrae naturally lose moisture and elasticity, making them more vulnerable to degeneration, bulging, and herniation. Excess weight and smoking can accelerate this process,” Anand added.

In addition to pain at individual body sites, the researchers examined three broader measures: any bodily pain, high intensity pain, and generalized pain.

Any bodily pain was defined as pain reported at any site. High intensity pain was defined as an average pain intensity of 7 or higher on a 0–10 scale. Generalized pain was defined as pain occurring in 4 of 5 body regions according to the 2016 fibromyalgia spatial criteria.

The prevalence of any bodily pain increased progressively across the life span, from around 30% at age 5 to 70% among people ages 100 and older. All three pain measures were consistently higher in women than men.

While any bodily pain increased with age, high intensity and generalized pain peaked in mid-to-late life before declining.

High intensity pain peaked around age 50, while generalized pain peaked around age 70.

These different patterns could help identify when prevention and pain management efforts may be most beneficial across the life span.

Vernon Williams, MD, a sports neurologist and founding director of the Center for Sports Neurology and Pain Medicine at Cedars-Sinai Orthopedics and Sports Medicine in Los Angeles, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline there are several signs that pain should be evaluated by a doctor.

“People shouldn’t feel that they simply have to live with pain. I especially recommend talking with a doctor when pain persists, interferes with sleep or daily activities, limits exercise or function, or is accompanied by symptoms such as numbness, tingling, or weakness,” Williams said.

“Persistent pain deserves an evaluation to identify the underlying cause and develop an appropriate treatment plan.”

The researchers found that smoking, obesity, and lower household income together accounted for 18.3% of the global pain burden across all body sites.

People who currently smoked had a modest but consistently higher prevalence of pain across body sites from adolescence through age 65. The strongest association was seen with facial pain.

Obesity was increasingly associated with pain beginning in early adulthood, particularly in weight-bearing joints such as the knees, hips, feet, and ankles.

Lower household income was also associated with a higher prevalence of pain across all body sites beginning in early adulthood. The strongest association was seen with chest pain.

People with the lowest household incomes were more than twice as likely to report chest pain as those with the highest incomes. This difference was greatest in midlife and narrowed at older ages.

Williams noted that medication isn’t the only option for managing pain.

“Regular, appropriately paced exercise can help change how the brain experiences pain, while mindfulness, breathing exercises, adequate sleep, and other behavioral strategies can help the nervous system better regulate the pain response,” he said. “When medication is needed, I encourage patients to discuss non-opioid and non-pharmacologic options with their doctor as part of a comprehensive approach.”

Anand added that, “The best strategy remains prevention and preservation of function: maintaining a healthy weight, strengthening the muscles that support the joints, and avoiding smoking can all help reduce the strain placed on the musculoskeletal system.”

#4978. Taking Blood Pressure Meds? DASH Diet Likely Safe for Potassium Levels

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

People who adhere to the Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension (DASH) diet do not experience significant changes in potassium levels while taking blood pressure medications or nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory (NSAID) drugs, according to a new study.

Researchers also reported that people taking these medications do not experience significant changes in their kidney function.

Both types of medications are known to increase potassium levels. High potassium levels can lead to hyperkalemia, a condition that can cause issues with the heart rhythm. People with impaired kidney function due to chronic kidney disease are at increased risk of hyperkalemia.

“The DASH diet is a well-known, proven nutrition pattern that is known to improve blood pressure,” said Marian Budu, MD, a clinical research coordinator on the GoFresh Trials at Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center in Boston and the presenting author of the study, in a news release.

“Knowing that the DASH diet did not increase blood potassium levels in this population with normal baseline potassium levels provides assurance that the DASH diet can be prescribed to a wide range of people,” she continued.

The preliminary findings are being presented at the American Heart Association’s Hypertension Scientific Sessions 2026 meeting this week in Virginia. The research has not been published yet in a peer-reviewed journal.

Experts not involved in the study told Healthline that the research provides medical professionals with important information.

“While we have traditionally exercised caution before recommending the DASH diet in patients who are prescribed ACE inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers, or potassium-sparing diuretics, these results show that those patients could likely adopt the DASH diet without significant concern for harm,” said Cheng-Han Chen, MD, an interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA.

“DASH diets do not appear to increase potassium levels to any significant degree in patients taking medications that are known to potentially raise serum potassium,” added Kevin Volpp, MD, the founding director of the Penn Center for Health Incentives and Behavioral Economics in Pennsylvania and the scientific lead of the American Heart Association’s Health Care by Food initiative.

“This is important because it is a theoretical concern that diets rich in potassium could cause high levels in potassium in some patients.”

The new study was a secondary analysis of the GoFresh and GoFresh Rx randomized trials that enrolled Black adults in the greater Boston area with high blood pressure.

Some of the participants were taking medications to lower their blood pressure, and others were not taking any hypertension drugs.

The study focused on four classes of blood pressure medications: angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors (ACEi), angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs), and potassium-sparing diuretics and beta-blockers, as well as NSAIDs.

The analysis took place in 2026 and included 356 Black adults with an average age of 53. About 70% were women.

At the start of the study, the participants had systolic blood pressure readings between 120 mm Hg and less than 150 mm Hg. They also had a diastolic blood pressure reading of less than 100 mm Hg.

The participants also had varying degrees of kidney function. In addition, they had limited access to grocery stores.

Half of the participants received three months of weekly DASH-patterned groceries and weekly nutrition counseling sessions. The other half received a cash stipend every four weeks to buy groceries, with no nutrition counseling.

The participants’ blood potassium levels were measured at the beginning of the study and again after 12 weeks.

The researchers reported that eating more potassium-rich foods in the DASH diet did not increase potassium levels to dangerous levels among participants taking blood pressure medications.

They noted no significant increase in potassium levels, regardless of whether participants were assigned to the dietary intervention or the monetary stipend group.

In a news release, the researchers acknowledged that their study did not include people with stage 4 or stage 5 kidney disease or those with higher-than-normal potassium levels.

They added that the study population comprised only Black individuals living in Boston, so the results may not generalize to the broader population.

The study authors said that future research should focus on participants with suboptimal kidney function and those with higher potassium levels.

Volpp agreed that more research is needed.

“This study was conducted in patients with non-elevated potassium levels at baseline and does not answer the question of whether they are safe in patients with elevated potassium levels or significant impairments in renal function,” he told Healthline.

Chen noted that potassium levels should be taken seriously.

“Certain classes of blood pressure medications can potentially cause high potassium levels, especially in people with kidney disease,” he told Healthline. “If potassium levels become too high, this can lead to symptoms such as fatigue, nausea, and muscle pain, or even serious heart problems such as arrhythmias.”

The DASH diet is a dietary plan that emphasizes fruits, vegetables, and lean proteins such as chicken, fish, and beans.

The plan also includes whole grains, low fat dairy products, and cooking with vegetable oils. The diet discourages the intake of red meat, salt, fat, and added sugars.

Previous research has indicated that the DASH diet can provide numerous health benefits.

A preliminary 2022 study concluded that the dietary plan can help lower blood pressure and improve heart health, but those results haven’t been published in a scientific journal. Still, the findings were consistent with a 2019 study that found the DASH diet can help lower the risk of heart disease.

In addition, a 2020 study reported that adults aged 65 and older with obesity lost body fat while following the DASH Diet. A 2023 study suggested the diet can help reduce belly fat.

A 2019 meta-analysis suggested that the DASH diet may also help lower the risk of certain cancers.

Many of these protective effects are attributed to the diet’s high fruit and vegetable content.

Chen said the DASH diet has many benefits, but the eating plan might not be for everybody.

“The DASH diet has been shown to be beneficial in lowering blood pressure, losing weight, and controlling blood sugar and cholesterol levels,” he said. “However, the amount of planning and portion tracking required to follow the diet may be too much for some people.”

Chen recommended that people ease into the DASH diet.

“The best way to start on the DASH diet is to slowly introduce the changes to your meals,” he said. “This can be as simple as including a few extra servings of fruits and vegetables each day as well as swapping some whole grain products for refined grains. Over time, more of the diet can be incorporated.”

Volpp agreed that introducing the DASH diet in increments is a good strategy.

“For many people, it is easiest to make small incremental changes sequentially,” he said. “One way to get started is to increase fruit by a couple of servings a day. Another is to pay attention to the amount of sodium in packaged foods and consciously choose products lower in sodium. A third is to make a conscious effort to eat more vegetables.”

“Make changes first that are easiest for you to make; it will be easier to make those changes stick,” Volpp added.

#4973. Ford’s new sub-$30K electric truck can power your home for a lot less

Ford wants to make powering your home with an EV a lot cheaper. Its upcoming sub-$30,000 Fathom electric truck will feature a new bidirectional charging system that ditches the need for an expensive external inverter.

Arriving in 2027, the Fathom will be the first EV from a US automaker priced below $30,000 that can deliver more than 10 kW of AC power to a home without a separate wall-mounted inverter.

And Ford says it’s cutting the cost of the other equipment needed for home backup power, too.

EV batteries store electricity as direct current (DC), but our homes use alternating current (AC). To send power from an EV battery into a house, traditionally, that meant installing a pricey external inverter.

Ford has come up with a different approach for its new Universal EV Platform, which will underpin the Fathom.

After acquiring startup Auto Motive Power in 2023, Ford redesigned its bidirectional charging system from the ground up. Instead of relying on a separate wall-mounted inverter, it built one directly into the truck’s E-box, one of the vehicle’s five main modules.

Fewer parts mean lower costs, and Ford says the savings will extend to the home charging equipment, too.

Fathom owners will have two ways to use the truck’s battery to power their homes.

Home Power Management will let owners charge their truck when electricity rates are low, then use that stored power at home when rates are higher. That could mean real savings for households on time-of-use electricity plans. They’ll need Ford’s new Home Power Station, which the company expects to cost about half as much as its first-generation bidirectional charger.

Home Backup Power will let the truck keep the lights on during an outage. For that, owners will need both the Home Power Station and Ford’s Home Backup Panel. Ford says the backup hardware should cost at least 60% less than its first-generation equipment.

Ford hasn’t released prices for either piece of hardware, so we don’t yet know what a complete home installation will cost.

But pairing a sub-$30,000 electric truck with substantially cheaper home power equipment could make bidirectional charging a lot more appealing.

Making the hardware cheaper is one thing. Making sure utilities can handle thousands of EVs that send electricity back into homes and potentially the grid is another.

Ford formed a working group in July with American Electric Power, BC Hydro, Consumers Energy, Exelon, and Pacific Gas and Electric. They’re working on making it easier for customers to connect bidirectional EVs before the Fathom arrives next year.

For example, someone using their truck solely for home backup power during an outage shouldn’t have to go through the same approval process as someone sending electricity to the grid.

The group is also looking at how to update utility procedures to accommodate newer bidirectional charging standards and make it easier for EV owners to participate in virtual power plants (VPPs). In other words, the batteries in parked EVs could become a useful source of electricity when the grid needs it most.

Ford says the Fathom’s system is designed to meet applicable UL 1741 requirements and IEEE 1547 interconnection standards. It also uses SAE J3068 communications technology, which Ford says helps reduce charger costs.

The idea is to get the technical requirements and utility processes sorted out before affordable bidirectional EVs start arriving in much larger numbers.

Affordable EVs already offer some form of bidirectional power. Two of the cheapest electric cars in the US, the Chevy Bolt and Nissan Leaf, are both available for under $30,000.

The Bolt supports vehicle-to-home (V2H) power through GM Energy equipment, while the Leaf offers vehicle-to-load (V2L) capability through an external device plugged into its charging port.

Ford’s system takes things much further. It can deliver more than 10 kW of AC power to a home without an expensive external inverter, and Ford says the rest of the equipment will cost considerably less, too.

That’s a pretty compelling proposition, especially for people who live in areas prone to power outages or have electricity plans with higher peak-hour rates.

When power outages last a while the area still affected shrinks. While a whole county may be out day one, by day 3 it may just be your neighborhood left. At that point you could drive across town and recharge the car and be good to power your house for another few days.

There are still some unknowns. Ford hasn’t announced the final hardware prices, and installation costs will vary. Plus, utility rules need to catch up if EV owners are going to take full advantage of bidirectional charging.

But this seems to be going in the right direction. If automakers can bring costs down and the industry can agree on consistent standards, having an EV that doubles as a home battery could eventually become a normal part of EV ownership rather than an expensive bonus feature.
