#4970. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: Lung Cancer Can Look Like Anyone

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Picture someone with lung cancer. Now picture a 24-year-old soccer player in the prime of his life. A filmmaker in her early 40s with a camera in her hand, doing what she loves best. A busy mom of two, with a shoulder so sore she can’t pick up her newborn, yet struggling to get a diagnosis.

The gap between the picture in our heads and the people actually being diagnosed is costing time. And time is the one thing with lung cancer you cannot get back.

We know that lung cancer is the most common cancer worldwide and the leading cause of cancer death. And while lung cancer incidence rates are falling in many countries as fewer people smoke, lung cancer in people who have never smoked is on the rise globally, both as a proportion and in absolute numbers.

According to the CDC, as many as 20% of lung cancers diagnosed in the US are in people who have never smoked. Data suggests that non-smoking lung cancer is now among the most common causes of cancer-related deaths worldwide.

The numbers are stark. Lung cancer can affect anyone. And that includes the young – like Alex, one of the subjects of a powerful new short documentary commissioned by the charity EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK for their Our Lungs: Time to Breathe campaign.

Alex was a 24-year-old soccer player who died just weeks after receiving a diagnosis of Epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) positive lung cancer, following several misdiagnoses born of the fact that no one suspected lung cancer in one so young, fit, and with no smoking history. The film was made by filmmaker Sarah Li, who herself was diagnosed with Stage 4 EGFR+ lung cancer in April 2024, just a month after Alex died from the same mutation of the disease, and charts her journey with survivor’s guilt, what an earlier diagnosis may have meant for Alex, and what that fact means for her own future.

Ahead of Lung Cancer Awareness Month in November, I sat down with my friend, Gini Harrison, to talk about the film, her own journey, and why catching lung cancer sooner matters so much. Gini is a Professor of Psychology at The Open University, a trustee of EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK, and is living with stage 4 EGFR-positive lung cancer. She’s also the mom at the top of this piece, whose sore shoulder was initially attributed to breastfeeding posture.

This interview has been lightly edited for clarity and length.

Gini Harrison: I’d had my son, Michael, in February 2021 and was on maternity leave. My daughter, Emily, was five. I think the shoulder pain started around March, but that was it. No cough, no breathlessness… nothing that made me think about my lungs.

Initially, they gave me painkillers, which helped a bit. But the pain kept getting worse. Eventually, I started losing movement and strength in my arm, and it got to the point where I couldn’t pick up my son anymore.

This was all happening during COVID, so my appointments were over the telephone. Eventually, after months without being examined face-to-face, I paid to see a shoulder specialist. As soon as he touched my shoulder blade and I practically leaped out of the chair, he knew something wasn’t right. He ordered an MRI, which showed a tumor in my shoulder blade and another in my upper right lung. In December 2021, I was diagnosed with stage IV lung cancer.

Gini Harrison: I wasn’t thinking, “This must be cancer.” I was just thinking, “Why isn’t this getting better?”

And I think that distinction matters. Patients shouldn’t have to identify their own disease before they can get help. You should be able to say that something is wrong and be listened to.

Gini Harrison: It was devastating. So many of my first questions were about the children: “How are we going to tell them?” “Am I going to be here to see them grow up?” “Will they remember me?” Suddenly, there was a question mark over a future I’d taken for granted.

I was also completely shocked by the type of cancer. If someone had said breast cancer, I don’t think I would have been anywhere near as surprised. But lung cancer?! I’d always associated it with smoking, and I’d never smoked.

I hadn’t imagined that this particular cancer could ever happen to me. So yes, I had some assumptions that I now challenge. I understand how deeply rooted they are because I had them too.

Gini Harrison: People are often shocked, and one of the first questions is whether I smoked.

And they often look healthy. That doesn’t mean everything is easy, or that there aren’t side effects. It just means you can’t tell what someone is dealing with by looking at them.

I think we’ve given lung cancer such a narrow public image that people outside it can become almost invisible.

Gini Harrison: Oh, yes! Once, I spoke to a doctor about a breathing problem after radiotherapy. He asked, “How much did you smoke?” When I said I’d never smoked, he replied, “Well, how did you get lung cancer then?”

On another occasion, I went to a hospice for help managing side effects, and someone there asked, “How are you still alive?” That’s quite a thing to hear when you’ve come looking for support!

When people ask about smoking, I try to explain that I’ve never smoked and I’m certainly not alone. Never smoking doesn’t make you immune. But I’m also very clear that nobody deserves lung cancer, whether they’ve smoked or not. We shouldn’t be creating a hierarchy of who deserves compassion.

Smoking history matters medically. But in a personal conversation, “How are you doing?” is a much kinder place to start.

Gini Harrison: Lung cancer isn’t one single disease. Two people can both have lung cancer, but different changes in their cells may be driving it.

Genes contain the instructions that tell our cells how to work, including when to grow and divide. Changes in those genes (called mutations) can cause cells to keep dividing when they shouldn’t, forming a tumor. In my case, there’s a mutation in a gene called EGFR.

EGFR-positive lung cancer is a type of non-small cell lung cancer caused by a genetic mutation in the EGFR gene, which keeps the EGFR receptor switched “on,” leading to uncontrolled cell growth and enhanced cancer cell survival.

Biomarker testing looks for these changes, as well as other features of the cancer that can guide treatment. If we know what’s helping the cancer grow, there may be a medication that specifically targets it.

The word “genetic” can sound scary, particularly when you have children. But these changes usually develop in cancer cells over a person’s lifetime. They aren’t necessarily inherited.

Gini Harrison: Please get it checked. And if it persists, worsens or doesn’t improve with treatment, go back. Explain what’s changed and how it’s affecting your life. Ask, “What happens next if this doesn’t get better?”

The symptoms don’t always look like lung symptoms. Mine was shoulder pain.

Most symptoms will turn out to be something other than cancer. But when a symptom continues to worsen, the original explanation needs to be reconsidered. Lower risk doesn’t mean no risk.

And I don’t want all the responsibility to fall on patients to keep pushing. Reassurance should come with a next step.

Gini Harrison: Once I had recovered from my initial treatment, I realized that I wanted to help others in my situation, so I became a trustee of the charity EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK. Through this work, we produced a film, Our Lungs: Time to Breathe. At the heart of it, it challenges the perception of what someone with lung cancer looks like.

The hardest part is how close to home it feels. Alex was so young, and that worked against him. Watching his family talk about what happened is heartbreaking. It’s just so unfair.

Gini Harrison: Sarah’s story shows life continuing alongside lung cancer. She’s a filmmaker with ambitions, people she loves, and things she wants to do.

Through Alex’s family, you see the enormity of the loss. I hope people feel that, rather than simply hearing another statistic.

Gini Harrison: For me, the biggest thing is that my children are growing up and I’m here to see it. Michael is now five (the age Emily was when I was diagnosed), and Emily is about to turn ten. I can’t tell you how grateful I am to be here with them.

It wasn’t about big bucket-list things. It was about being here every day. Seeing Michael start school. Seeing Emily reach double figures. Those milestones are enormous when you’ve wondered whether you’d get to see them.

After chemotherapy and radiotherapy, I’ve had no evidence of disease since May 2022, and my scans remain stable. But a big change has been seeing the progress in available treatment. It gives me more hope about what might be available if I need treatment again.

The public image of lung cancer still needs to catch up. We need better recognition of symptoms, access to appropriate testing and treatment, and support for the whole person. As a psychologist as well as a patient, I’m particularly aware that psychological well-being and family life need attention alongside the cancer itself.

Gini Harrison: I’d love for people to share the film and help get it in front of those who wouldn’t normally think lung cancer has anything to do with them. And take something from it into everyday life: book the appointment you’ve been putting off, ask your doctor about testing, or respond with kindness when someone tells you about their diagnosis.

Just being willing to rethink your assumptions could make a difference.

Gini Harrison: “You don’t have to put a brave face on with me. How are you really doing?”

When you look well, or your scans are good, people can assume you’re “fine now”. But I’m not sure the fear ever completely goes away. Every scan is terrifying. It feels like there’s a sword of Damocles hanging over my head, and part of me is waiting for the bad scan. I also still have lasting side effects from treatment, and probably always will.

My life has fundamentally changed. Some of that has brought good things, but it doesn’t make the difficult parts disappear. It helps when someone gives you room to be honest about both, without expecting you to reassure them or turn everything into a positive story.

Gini and I have been friends for nearly 30 years, and, while she’d probably cringe to hear me say it, I remain in awe of the way she approaches life with cancer. I am so grateful for her sharing her experiences with me for this column. What Gini asks of us is simple: look again, ask the question, listen, and act on your instincts. Our Lungs: Time to Breathe is where that starts for her. You can learn more about the Our Lungs: Time to Breathe campaign and the wider work of EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK by clicking the links. — Robin Hough

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#4968. BYD names its ultra-luxury EV, and it could be a game changer next week

BYD’s new flagship EV will be its largest and most luxurious electric sedan yet, potentially debuting two firsts for the company next week: Rolls-Royce-style coach doors and solid-state batteries.

BYD is better known for its low-cost electric cars, like the Seagull, a little hatchback that sells for under $10,000 in China.

The Chinese auto giant has greatly expanded its portfolio over the past few months and years. BYD now offers everything from entry-level EVs to three-row flagship SUVs, pickup trucks, electric supercars, and just about everything in between.

BYD has three sub-brands: Denza, FangChengBao, and Yangwang. It also has two main families under the BYD brand: the Dynasty and Ocean series.

Yangwang sits at the top of BYD’s brand hierarchy and showcases its latest breakthrough technologies.

Previously, Yangwang vehicles debuted on BYD’s quad-motor e4 platform, delivering a whopping 1,300 horsepower and featuring its DiSus-X intelligent body control system, which enables the vehicle to “dance,” jump, and drive on three wheels.

For its next model, BYD is pushing further into the elite segment to take on ultra-luxury brands like Rolls-Royce, Bentley, and Mercedes-Maybach.

BYD revealed the name of its flagship ultra-luxury EV for the first time on Tuesday: the Yangwang Altior, along with a new teaser of the partially uncovered executive sedan.

Coming soon#YANGWANG #YANGWANGALTIOR pic.twitter.com/kFnu6X6csq

Yangwang general manager Hu Xiaoqing said the name was chosen as an independent Chinese-English naming scheme. In China, it goes by DingYao. The “Ding” symbolizes stability and endurance, while “Yao” signifies radiant brilliance, or shining brightly like a star in the sky.

The English name Altior comes from Latin for “higher,” representing Yangwang’s relentless pursuit of ever-greater heights.

The new teaser comes after Hu revealed several images of the new Yangwang executive sedan testing for the first time last month.

Although it was disguised, it appeared to be about the same shape and size as the Mercedes-Benz Maybach S-Class or Rolls-Royce Phantom. Local reports indicate it will be around 5,500 mm (216″) long.

HU confirmed for the first time last week that the global launch of the new flagship sedan will take place in the UAE, not in BYD’s home market of China.

BYD has not yet revealed a specific launch date, but the Yangwang sedan could make its first appearance as early as next week. According to CarNewsChina, citing “sources familiar with the project,” BYD is aiming to reveal the new flagship model in Dubai, UAE, around November 5.

Zheng Yu, Yangwang’s executive general manager, said the new model will feature “unprecedented disruptive technology,” but didn’t reveal specifics.

Although details are still limited, the ultra-luxury sedan was shown at BYD’s Flash Charging station, suggesting it will support megawatt flash charging.

It also features rear-hinged coach doors, a first for BYD. The flagship EV will reportedly be the first to feature BYD’s solid-state batteries.

BYD executive vice president Stella Li said during an interview with Carwow.es last month that the company’s first vehicle to use solid-state batteries will debut in 2027.

Li said BYD is “in the leading position” in solid-state battery technology and its commercialization, and the company plans to prove it next year.

BYD’s battery unit, FinDreams, plans to begin production of sulfide-based all-solid-state batteries in 2027. It will initially be produced in limited batches, then scale to mass production closer to the end of the decade.

The part about solid state batteries got my attention. I'm looking forward to learning more about how they perform!

While BYD has yet to confirm it, introducing the new battery tech in a flagship model would make sense.

The executive sedan is expected to debut around November 5. Prices are rumored to start in the 2 million yuan range, or about $300,000.

Yangwang’s current electric sedan, the U7, starts at 658,000 yuan ($98,000). The brand also sells the U8 (and U8L), a plug-in hybrid luxury off-road SUV, and the U9, a fully electric super-coupe.

The extended U8L is Yangwang’s highest-priced mass-produced vehicle, starting at 1.45 million yuan ($217,000).

#4967. How Big of a Calorie Deficit Do You Need to Achieve Body Recomp?

Your calorie deficit could be working against you. Here’s how to find the right balance.

With body recomposition come two goals: lose body fat while gaining muscle. At first glance, it might seem a bit too good to be true, if you think about how each goal is achieved individually. But it’s not.

To lose body fat, you need to eat fewer calories than you burn in a day. To gain muscle, you need to continuously challenge your muscles in the gym, and eat enough to hit your protein goals, which is vital to building muscle mass—especially in a calorie deficit.

With the two goals seemingly at nutritional odds, it’s difficult to determine exactly how you should eat to hit this two-for-one goal. Exactly how big of a calorie deficit do you need to achieve body recomp? We tapped an expert to find out.

Get your personalized plan with our Eating for Body Recomp guide

Achieving body recomp takes some strategic nutritional planning, starting with a balanced calorie goal.

Keep your calorie deficit on the more conservative side when it comes to body recomp so you have enough energy to power your important workouts, Dana White, RD, previously told WH. Aim for a calorie deficit no larger than 250 to 500 calories per day. Any larger than that, and you run the risk of feeling under-fueled in the gym or not hitting your protein goals—which is major for building and maintaining muscle mass.

With body recomp, though, it’s important to get even more granular and understand what’s making up those cals, says White. “It’s not just about eating calories—it’s the type of calories.”

One of the best ways to determine how to fill those calories is by tracking your macronutrients—carbohydrates, proteins, and fats. To find out how much of each macro you should get, check out our Eating for Body Recomp guide.

Cori Ritchey, C.S.C.S., is the fitness editor at Women's Health, as well as a certified strength and condition coach and group fitness instructor. She’s reported on topics regarding health, nutrition, mental health, fitness, sex, and relationships for several years. You can find more of her work in Men’s Health, HealthCentral, Livestrong, Self, and others.

#4965. Musk declares Tesla Robotaxi win over Waymo, but Waymo still drives 7x more

Elon Musk says Tesla’s Robotaxi race against Waymo “was never a real contest” after Tesla’s driverless fleet logged 438,000 miles in Austin in a single week, edging past Waymo’s 406,000 weekly average in the city.

That’s one city, one week, and a Waymo figure that’s four months old. Across the US, Waymo still drives about seven times more driverless miles than Tesla every week.

The chart going around comes from Robotaxi Tracker, a community project that pulls driverless mileage from each company’s own reports: Tesla’s weekly Robotaxi safety report and Waymo’s quarterly Safety Impact data hub.

Jason Scharf posted the Austin view on X with the comment that “the battle for Autonomous Austin might already be over.” Musk replied overnight: “It was never a real contest.”

The Austin data is real –albeit with some caveats. In the week ending October 18, Tesla reported 438,000 miles with no safety monitor aboard in Austin, up 30% from the week before. Waymo averaged about 406,000 miles a week in Austin in the second quarter.

But the tracker’s own caption explains the catch. Tesla’s figure is its latest week. Waymo’s is an average of April through June, because Waymo publishes about three months after each quarter ends. So Musk is comparing Tesla’s best week ever to Waymo’s spring.

Switch the same chart to “All markets” and the story flips.

Waymo averaged 3.90 million rider-only miles per week in Q2 2026. Tesla did 558,000 miles in its best week. Waymo’s Phoenix, Bay Area, and Los Angeles markets each drive more miles per week on their own than Tesla’s entire network.

Cumulative numbers are even more lopsided. Waymo has logged 271.3 million rider-only miles since 2020, including 50.7 million in Q2 alone, as we covered when Waymo released its latest safety data. Tesla has 3.11 million driverless miles since January 22, 2026. Waymo drove more than that in a single week.

And Tesla’s total leans almost entirely on one city:

Austin accounts for 79% of Tesla’s weekly driverless miles. Houston, Tampa, Orlando, and Miami combined did about 25,000 miles. Waymo’s smallest tracked market, Atlanta, averaged 250,000 a week in Q2.

The Austin surge has an obvious cause. Tesla launched public Cybercab rides in Austin on September 3.

According to Robotaxi Tracker’s count of the Texas DMV automated-vehicle list, Tesla now has 463 Cybercabs registered in Texas, 337 of them added in the last 30 days. That’s 48% of the 970 autonomous vehicles Tesla has registered in the state.

Tesla isn’t hiding the strategy. On the Q3 earnings call last week, Ashok Elluswamy, Tesla’s head of AI software, said the company wants to “swarm Austin with Cybercabs everywhere.” Musk said the Austin Cybercab fleet had grown “by a factor of 10” in about a month.

So Tesla dumped hundreds of new, company-owned cars into one metro and the miles went up. That’s fleet deployment. It doesn’t show the software can do what Waymo does in 15 cities, and Tesla can’t repeat it everywhere at once.

The bigger problem is what the cars are allowed to do.

On the same earnings call, Elluswamy described the service as constrained by the software, not by regulators. He said that if there’s “the slightest possible risk at all,” Tesla doesn’t take it on, and that the limits are “technology driven” for now. Service hours only recently stretched from 9 p.m. toward 2 a.m., and he listed highways, “higher speed roads,” 24/7 operation, and more weather conditions as things Tesla still has to unlock.

You can see those limits in the rides. Tesla Robotaxis don’t take highways, and riders report they avoid at-grade railroad crossings. In September, a rider had a 10-minute Cybercab trip in Austin turn into a 70-minute detour because the car drove south through East Austin until it found a bridge over the rail line, instead of taking MoPac.

That matters for this comparison, too. Miles is a flattering metric when your car takes the long way. A trip that should cover a few miles and instead loops across town logs a lot more mileage for the same rider.

Waymo, meanwhile, has been taking riders on freeways in Phoenix, San Francisco, and Los Angeles since November 2025.

Credit where it’s due: Tesla’s driverless miles are growing fast, 30% in a single week, and Austin now has a real Tesla fleet carrying the public without anyone in the driver’s seat. A year ago, that wasn’t the case.

I continue to hear tesla refer to its miles as 'driverless miles', but Waymo's as rider only miles. This can be a big difference. Are you sure that this is not an apples and oranges comparison? Do we have confidence that Tesla is only including miles with a rider inside and not driverless miles where the can is spinning circles in a parking lot??

But “never a real contest” is a strange thing to say when you’re behind by 7x on weekly miles and 87x on cumulative miles, and your one win depends on comparing your newest week to your rival’s data from last spring.

What stands out to us is the type of autonomy Tesla is deploying. In 2020, Musk wrote that “Waymo is impressive, but a highly specialized solution,” and that Tesla’s approach was “a general solution.” For years, Tesla fans dismissed Waymo as a geofenced system that only worked in mapped areas with lots of restrictions.

Now look at Tesla’s Robotaxi: geofenced, no highways, avoiding railroad crossings, taking 70 minutes for 10-minute trips, and limited by hours because the software isn’t ready for anything with “the slightest possible risk.” By Musk’s own old definition, that’s a highly specialized solution. Waymo is the one driving on freeways now.

Robotaxis still need electricity, and so does your EV. If you drive electric, pairing it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer.
